#6742. Inside a Denver Tudor That Blends English Eccentricity With Family-Friendly Design

Refurbished original details give the renovation a touch of old-soul charm.

When designer Kaitlyn Hoffman and her family returned to the United States after eight years in London, they had grown accustomed to the tighter quarters that city living often dictates. Needless to say, settling into a five-bedroom Tudor in Denver’s Cheesman Park neighborhood was a big leap. Built in 1900, the house was in good condition when Hoffman and her husband bought it, but earlier renovations had left it feeling at odds with its architectural style.

“The biggest design challenge was anticipating how we would actually live in the home,” Hoffman says. Because they renovated the space before moving in, she had to guess at everything from where they’d gather to how the house would work for their two young kids. “Ultimately, it was an exercise in designing for a life we hadn’t quite experienced yet and trusting our instincts about how we wanted to live.”

The nine-month project, which also launched her firm, Studio Proper Design, restored the home’s Tudor character. Hoffman and her partners at Alter Design + Build gutted the primary and powder bathrooms, overhauled the kitchen, and made smaller updates almost everywhere else. Along the way, plenty of the home’s charming original details got a second life: A pair of French doors found in the basement now anchors a custom pantry, and diamond-pattern mullions in the primary bath windows make the room look as though it had always been part of the house.

The result is a home that reflects Hoffman’s signature “Brit-ish” style: layered, collected, and not too precious. “I think the magic is in the mix,” she says, crediting her years abroad with inspiring a fondness for eccentricity, coziness, and humor in her design projects. Even the house’s lore fits the mood. Neighbors insist it’s the former home of ‘60s country-rock group Nitty Gritty Dirt Band—though Hoffman admits she’s never been able to confirm it. “Whether it’s true or not, it’s fun to imagine that these walls might have witnessed a little rock-and-roll history.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Kaitlyn Hoffman of Studio Proper DesignDesign Partner: Alter Design + BuildStylist: Natalie WaradyLocation: Denver, ColoradoThe Space: A five-bedroom, three-and-a-half-bathroom Tudor across 6,345 square feet

A place where the whole family gathers and hosts, this living room is packed with personality and beloved furnishings, many of which have changed hands in the family throughout the years. While the decor is decidedly eclectic, the architecture itself is classic and stately. “We wanted this room to have the elegance of a traditional English drawing room, so we introduced egg-and-dart crown molding and a hand-carved Louis XV–style marble fireplace to give the space a greater sense of formality and architectural presence,” Hoffman shares.

According to Hoffman, this area saw a true “everything but the kitchen cabinets” renovation. Existing wall ovens gave way to a freestanding range with a custom hood, and a new pantry took their place. It’s built around a pair of original French doors she found in the basement, adding key storage while hiding away the appliances.

To soften the modern lines, she added a paneled backsplash, unlacquered brass hardware, and sealed walnut butcher block countertops for a warm, rustic touch. “I wanted a kitchen that worked hard for our family but felt every bit as inviting as the rest of the house,” Hoffman adds.

Though the dining room shares space with the living room, both areas needed to have distinct identities. To make guests feel like they’re entering into a completely separate room, Hoffman sourced several statement-making pieces. “I found all six antique Louis-style dining chairs on Facebook Marketplace for just $100, while the mirrored faux-bamboo armoire is a favorite piece we brought back with us from London,” Hoffman says.

Tucked beneath the stairs between the kitchen and living room is a clever nook for mixing up the drink of the hour. “This multipurpose space houses my husband’s (many!) coffee supplies by day and transforms into a bar by night,” Hoffman says. Like in the kitchen, she preserved the existing cabinetry and gave it a fresh coat of blue paint, new Negresco honed granite countertops, wallpaper, and updated fixtures. Now, it rivals the kitchen as the go-to entertaining spot in the home.

While gut-renovating this powder room, Hoffman fell in love with the botanical wallpaper, and she would’ve covered the entire space in it if it were a little more kid-friendly. “I opted for wainscoting on the lower half of the walls to protect the wallpaper from sticky fingers,” she says.

This is one of two generously sized guest bedroom suites, so friends and family will never feel short-changed when it’s time to hit the hay. It also has some of the most intriguing decorative details in the entire house. “The gingham four-poster bed is the star of the room, and I love the playful contrast between its bold check and the floral Roman shades,” she says. The wall accents are equally as enviable. “The framed Hermès scarf is another longtime favorite, which I sourced at Round Top over a decade ago, before Round Top was cool!”

Hoffman took a light-touch approach to her daughter’s room, knowing that the seven-year-old would eventually want to put her own stylistic signature on it. “I wanted to create a room that felt charming and playful without being overly childish, with pieces she could continue to enjoy as she grows,” she notes. Beyond painting the walls a pretty blush pink, the only real change here was adding custom Roman shades, which give the room a more tailored, finished feel.

This primary bathroom saw a full gut renovation to bring it up to the standards of modern living. Along with adding his-and-hers sinks and a freestanding tub, Hoffman took the opportunity to correct an architectural eyesore: the two mismatched windows that created irritating asymmetry. “We ultimately replaced both windows, incorporating diamond-pattern mullions in the upper third to complement the home’s Tudor architecture,” she adds. “It was a significant expense, but it gave us greater flexibility with the layout and brought a sense of continuity to the room.”

This guest room is an homage to the family’s life in London, as much of the decor came from their former home across the pond. The art, too, was sourced from a local printmaker whose studio was down the street in the family’s old neighborhood. Any new additions here were smaller and strategic. “The Schumacher floral fabric on the curtains and pillows became the thread that tied everything together, bringing color, pattern, and a sense of cohesion to the room,” Hoffman says.

The kids’ bathroom was reworked with total flexibility in mind. “With a son and a daughter sharing the space, I wanted a palette that felt playful and fresh without leaning either too feminine or masculine,” Hoffman notes. While the existing floor tile stayed, she added a custom double vanity so both kids can brush their teeth at the same time, as well as painted beadboard wainscoting to extend the country-home atmosphere into the washroom.

Kaitlyn Hoffman is the founder of Studio Proper, a boutique residential interior design firm located in Denver, Colorado. She’s a British citizen and lifelong Anglophile whose signature style was honed over nearly a decade living in London. Her design sensibility takes inspiration from traditional English interiors but adds a modern, practical twist.

#6738. John Mulaney’s Roast of New York Is an Instant SNL All-Timer

Veronika Slowikowska has some serious pipes, by the way.

It’s been an incredible year in New York City. The start of Zohran Mamdani’s mayoralship set a joyful tone that underscored the city’s immaculate vibe these last few months: the Knicks’ championship win, Harry Styles’ MSG residency, and a Saturday Halloween/Sunday marathon double whammy. So leave it to John Mulaney to take the city down a peg.

In the latest Saturday Night Live, the sketch of the night was easily “Central Park Horse & Carriage Ride.” Mulaney plays an off-kilter horse-cab driver (“You just trust me implicitly? You realize I have a top hat and a whip, right?”) who shows two tourists, played by Kam Patterson and Marcello Hernández, the best and worst of New York. With the impending ban on horse-drawn carriages in Central Park slated for June 2028, this is among the last times anyone can tour the city like this.

The sketch is unofficially a tribute to Broadway—a New York cultural staple—with short songs that pay homage to shows like Little Shop of Horrors, Funny Girl, Annie Get Your Gun, and West Side Story. The buzzy Oh, Mary! gets a major tip of the hat too, with current star Bowen Yang making a surprise return to 30 Rockefeller Plaza.

After a mandatory number pointing out that Central Park isn’t safe at night, you get Kenan Thompson as a weed cloud, Tommy Brennan as a rival pedicab cyclist (“I’ll take you to the MoMA/Put you in a coma” is a bar, I’m sorry), and a genuine crowd-pleasing duet with James Austin Johnson and Jeremy Culhane who sing “about a boy named Mamdani.” And there’s Yang, as “Mary Todd Lincoln Tunnel,” who sings a cover of “And I Am Telling You” about commuting through New Jersey.

But really, the biggest showstopper of the sketch and frankly all of Saturday Night Live this week is VERONIKA SLOWIKOWSKA. As a marathon runner who still hasn’t finished the race, Slowikowska shows off her golden pipes in a cover/parody of “Don’t Rain on My Parade.” Plainly, she crushes it. It’s moments like these that make you remember that SNL is full of overqualified, overly energetic theater kids now, and not stand-up comics blitzed out on cocaine.

You can watch the sketch in the embed below.

The sketch was maybe the best part of a pretty standout SNL, which was owed to the simple fact that Mulaney was the host. A former show writer turned seven-time guest host, Mulaney’s presence was essentially permission to let everyone’s hair down so they could take bigger swings. Mulaney himself said it: “I used to be a writer at this show, and one of the only difficult things about working here was dealing with the host. So it’s fun when I host, because it’s like there’s no host.” Kinda explains the sketch.

#6733. McDonald’s Just Brought Some Of Its Most Popular International Dishes To The U.S.

Why let other countries have all the fun?

You can find a McDonald's in over 100 countries, each offering unique international menu items. But you may not know that you can try some of them in the States—no passport required.

The restaurant at McDonald's Chicago headquarters has featured a special rotating menu of global favorites since opening in 2018. Its newest lineup has officially dropped, and it includes some dishes you don't want to miss.

The menu currently features two sandwiches. The first is Costa Rica's Mushroom Deluxe McCrispy, which comes with a crispy chicken fillet, cooked mushrooms, Swiss cheese, mayo, and crispy onions and garlic. You can also order a Chili Cheese McDouble from Germany that upgrades the classic burger with pickled jalapeños and a hot chili cheese sauce.

The Global Favorites menu wouldn't be complete without McNuggets, either. McDonald's is currently offering a version from Japan with spicy garlic black pepper seasoning.

Instead of the chain's classic fries, you can round out your global meal with Malaysian Twister Fries—otherwise known as curly fries. And to top it all off, there's a Strawberry Cheesecake McFlurry from Canada. (If there's any menu item that deserves a nationwide American debut, it's this one.)

Fast food fans reacted to the new menu with a combination of excitement and serious FOMO. "I'm moving to Chicago just to eat at the HQ full time," one Instagram user wrote. On X, another fan added, "Garlic black pepper nudges? You have my attention."

If you want to try any of these menu items for yourself, you'll have to visit the McDonald's headquarters restaurant at 1035 W. Randolph Street in Chicago. The Global Favorites menu rotates regularly, so don't put off your trip for too long.

#6732. Timothy Dalton's 007 Advice Is the Only Guidance Robert Pattinson Should Follow

The veteran actor notes that the true danger for the super-spy is trying to please everyone.

James Bond’s greatest weakness is not megalomaniacal madmen bent on world domination. Those he can handle. The superspy is far more likely to be taken down by well-meaning committees.

That’s why Robert Pattinson’s casting as filmmaker Denis Villeneuve’s new 007 is a bold and promising choice. The two of them together have amassed enough industry clout to craft the character their own way, to make him distinctive and daring in a way, hopefully, that we haven’t seen before.

Some things shouldn’t change, like his seductiveness, the Walther PPK, and the way he likes his martinis to be shaken. But those things only make up the silhouette of James Bond. Each actor must put his own imprint on the core of Ian Fleming’s debonair secret agent, just as the author did in his original novels. Sean Connery added ruggedness to the savoir faire. George Lazenby gave him a soulful vulnerability in his solo outing as the character. Roger Moore sharpened 007’s sense of humor. Each actor leaves his fingerprints behind as he hands it off to the next.

This leads to the essential question for anyone signing up to play James Bond: Are you strong enough to stand out?

It’s not so easily answered. I’ll let Timothy Dalton, my personal favorite Bond, explain: “I have some good friends from the Bond days, some really good people, but they’re trapped. Everyone’s trapped by it in a way,” he told me. “Whoever does it has got to have a very clear sense of what they want to achieve, because everybody in the world has got a different opinion about it.”

To figure out what comes next, we should look back at what came before.

Dalton (who happens to be Christopher Nolan’s favorite Bond too) made that comment to me back in February 2025, when Amazon MGM Studios first acquired the rights to the 007 character.

When he first took on the role in 1987’s The Living Daylights, his choice was to add a renegade wolf quality. But he also recognized that James Bond must remain, ultimately, the good guy, and that movie was entirely defined by an act of mercy. Fleming’s short story “The Living Daylights” makes up the film’s opening scene: Bond has been sent to take out an enemy sniper, but when he sees that his target is a beautiful cellist (played by Maryam d’Abo), he aims his own rifle to distract her rather than kill her.

He then becomes responsible for her life, and his 007 has no problem cutting down any foes who block their paths. Dalton’s Bond was more polished than Connery and less comedic than Moore, but he was led by a kind of stormy passion. He had heart, but it pumped cold blood.

Still, Dalton had to break through that formidable wall of consensus. Bond is not a solo enterprise, of course. The director has a say, as do the producers and the screenwriters, but the danger comes from two places: the studio and the audience itself. As Dalton said, “Everybody in the world has got a different opinion about it,” and those voices have to be defied, just as his 007 refused the order to place a bullet in the enemy cellist’s heart.

Nothing makes hands wring and sweat pour in a studio conference room like something unexpected, and know-it-all supposed “fans” often resist change while paradoxically demanding something fresh and new. He had two outings as Bond, the second being 1989’s License to Kill, in which 007 goes even more rogue, defying Her Majesty’s secret service to embark on a mission of revenge. Then the character became tied up in rights lawsuits for several years, and Dalton chose not to return.

When Daniel Craig took over 007 from Pierce Brosnan in 2005, purists complained about his blue eyes and blond hair. When they finally saw him bring the character to life in 2006’s Casino Royale, those critics soon fell silent. Craig gave Bond a soldier’s mindset. He was a battlefield veteran whose new uniform happens to be a dashing suit. He was successful for so long because as he added his own spin, he capitalized on the best of what came before: He was as rough as Connery and as lethal as Dalton. The humor and playfulness that defined Moore and Brosnan were still present, but they served as a mask for the kind of wounded vulnerability that Lazenby had introduced.

Pattinson is now free to pick and choose whatever qualities he likes from this Brotherhood of Bond, but if he is going to take anyone’s advice, it should be Dalton’s: “Whoever does it has got to have a very clear sense of what they want to achieve.”

Fortunately, no one tells Villeneuve what to do. The filmmaker has spent two decades establishing himself not just as a distinctive artist but as a reliable box-office draw. Those two qualities give him a powerful weapon—clout—that can stave off the onslaught of opinions that will inevitably surround this casting choice.

Just this Saturday, I moderated a Q&A with Villeneuve at the Golden Globe Foundation before an audience of students, and one young filmmaker named Oscar Flores asked the Dune filmmaker, “When is the right time for you to listen to feedback, and when is the right time to listen to yourself?”

Villeneuve gave an answer that perfectly matches Dalton’s advice and increases my optimism about his approach to Bond: “Honestly, people say that I’m humble, but that’s bullshit,” he said, only partly joking. “If I’m asking for $200 million for a movie, what kind of humility is that?”

The suggestion he made to the students was to not take on projects that are outside their control. Start small, stay true, and build up your skill set behind the camera while establishing a reputation that gives you defining power over the movies you make. He warned them not to follow the “siren” song of a big-budget offer if it means ceding control. Instead, Villeneuve said, he found it effective to build up his status with smaller films, moving from 2010’s more intimate Incendies to 2013’s harrowing Prisoners and 2015’s even more sprawling Sicario before leveling up in scale to 2016’s Arrival and 2017’s Blade Runner 2049.

“The thing that I did when I was a young filmmaker is that I tried not to jump steps,” he told us at the Golden Globes Foundation forum. “I went step-by-step on projects. I was able to keep control of them. I was making sure that I was in control of the tools, and control of the language, and getting strong with the crew, being able to and making sure that my voice would be heard. … What I will say to you is: Be careful of the sirens.”

Only after amassing this authority, Villeneuve said, did he feel the time was right to take on his dream project of Dune. “If you made a successful first feature film that cost $200,000 and they offer you a major blockbuster, be careful,” he said this weekend. “Be careful who you are working with, and don’t listen to your ego. Do you really believe that you have the skills to bring it to the screen? Maybe you do. Some people can, by the way. I was not ready to make Dune after my second [film]. ... It took me a lot of movies before I knew that I was able to handle the pressure and making sure that I would be able to control totally, 100 percent, the film. At the end of the day when I look at the movie and the screen, it’s my movie.”

No doubt Pattinson’s casting was his decision, and Villeneuve’s status ensured it was accepted by the powers that be at Amazon MGM Studios. It probably wasn’t a difficult yes, given that Pattinson is currently in his prime. Not only is he The Batman, but he stood out as the conniving usurper Antinous in Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey and has earned accolades for his haunting performance as a predator-hunting journalist in Primetime.

Is he a heartthrob? Ask the legion of Twilight fans, and cover your ears for their deafening reply.

Pattinson forged his bond with Villeneuve with another upcoming role in Dune: Part Three, playing the Face Dancer Scytale. He’s a shape-shifting psychological assassin who seeks to undermine Timothée Chalamet’s all-seeing, all-powerful emperor by getting inside his head, and the actor and filmmaker worked closely to figure out the look, mannerisms, and motivations of a creature void of form.

As Pattinson said in a recent cover story I wrote for Empire magazine about Dune: Part Three: “I went into it being like, ‘I want to look like an alien,’ and initially Denis was saying, ‘Well, I think he’s got to fit in with everybody. I think he just looks like you. He’s normal,’ ” Pattinson said. The actor was candid about his own reaction: “I was a little bit disappointed.”

Then Villeneuve thought about it some more. As the director told those students on Saturday, he has a strong point of view, but it’s not inflexible. Villeneuve is not interested in the whims of a crowd, but he is very open to suggestions from creative partners. If their own insights open his eyes to a possibility he hadn’t considered, he will seize upon the ideas.

Shortly before shooting, Villeneuve reversed course on Scytale, and Pattinson said the filmmaker sent him an email with a new look for the Face Dancer. Pale skin, white hair, no eyebrows, which has the effect of making his expressions somewhat inscrutable. In other words, a blank page, upon which the characteristics of others can be written as he shifts his own natural shape. Pattinson said he felt relief: “I’m like, ‘Okay, this is very much not like a normal guy at all.’ ”

This anecdote is meaningful for 007 because it shows that the filmmaker and the star can share ideas, experiment and explore, and listen to each other. That’s the collaboration this character needs.

At 40, Pattinson is not the youngest possible Bond, but he brings life experience that will be vital for playing a character who needs a certain prowess and wisdom to survive. He and Villeneuve both have a kind of double-0 swagger that will allow them to ignore the cacophonous advice of the masses and devise something original.

Pattinson has been doing this for a long time. Consider his doomed Cedric Diggory in 2005’s Harry Potter and the Goblet of Fire, another character—like Bond, Bruce Wayne, and Edward Cullen—who exists as an intense, preexisting image in the minds of millions of fans. But with his brooding presence, he added uncertainty to Cedric’s innate decency and fair-mindedness. The kid who dramatically hops down from a tree in his jump-scare introduction to Ron and Harry could very well have been there to bully them. That’s a sneak-attack entrance, proposed by Pattinson himself, and little details like that gave this upright, wholesome character a dose of intrigue.

Pattinson was only 17 when he filmed that. In the more than two decades that followed, he has paid his dues, learned his lessons, and no doubt gotten his share of scars. With Villeneuve backing him, he’s bulletproof. The two of them can take chances, be bold, and defy orders—just like 007 would, just as playing the character demands.

Pattinson is not a paper doll that will fold when the committees come for James Bond.

#6729. QQ: Exactly Whomst Has Joe Jonas Dated Over the Years?

Big news for JoBro fans everywhere: Joe Jonas is (reportedly) back on the market now that his yearlong romance with Tatiana Gabriela has come to an end. According to an Us Weekly insider, “Things started to shift as Joe got busier with work commitments and they were spending more time apart,” and though rumor has it that Joe “may have reconnected with a former girlfriend” (per Deuxmoi), he has yet to confirm any of this so...stand by for updates!

And if you’re wondering which of Joe’s former girlfriends he may have reconnected with, that’s fair, because there’s a lot of them! From Sophie Turner to Gigi Hadid to Taylor Swift, Joe has romanced his fair share of famous ladies, and the time has come to take a walk down memory lane. Meet ’em all here.

Joe and fellow former Disney star AJ Michalka dated way back in the day after the Jonas Brothers and her group, Aly & AJ, went on tour together. The two were a thing for about a year and a half before breaking it off, and AJ later admitted during a May 2026 appearance on Call Her Daddy that Joe was her first kiss.

“We met on tour,” she said of Joe. “The Jonas Brothers opened for us, and that’s when we started—like, there was a spark. I was like, ‘Oh, this would be fun.’ We started dating on that.”

Taylor Swift (you might’ve heard of her?) and Joe dated for roughly three months in 2008, but things did NOT end well. After they broke up, Taylor went on The Ellen Show and revealed that he'd dumped her during a 27-second phone call.

The two have since made up (she did send his baby a present, after all!), but that 27-second phone call will forever live in infamy.

Camilla Belle and Joe met when she starred in the Jonas Brothers’ “Lovebug” music video, and they began dating right after the musician ended things with Taylor Swift. Tay wasn’t too happy about the timing, and actually told Us Weekly, “They’ve been together since we broke up. That’s why we broke up—because he met her.”

FWIW, Camilla and Joe only dated for about seven months between October 2008 and July 2009 before going their separate ways, with a rep for Camilla saying, “There is no third party involved and they care deeply about each other and will remain friends.”

Joe met Demi Lovato on the set of the forever iconic Disney movie Camp Rock in 2007, but they didn’t start dating until 2010, and the relationship was shorter than you might have realized.

“We eventually dated for a month. I really got to know her and got to see the ins and outs of what she was struggling with, like drug abuse,” Joe later recounted to Vulture in 2013. “I felt like I needed to take care of her, but at the same time, I was living a lie because I wasn’t happy but felt like I had to stay in it for her because she needed help. I couldn’t express any of that, of course, because I had a brand to protect.”

Thankfully, both Demi and her relationship with Joe are in a much better place now.

Shortly after Joe and Demi ended things, Joe began dating Twilight star Ashley Greene in the summer of 2010. Joe called this his “first serious relationship,” and in that same 2013 Vulture interview, he explained why things went south: “We were together for almost a year. I was living out in L.A. by myself, and at the end of the day, long distance didn’t work. It’s incredibly difficult.”

What’s probably most, uhm, notable about this romance is that years later, during a Reddit AMA, Joe revealed that he lost his virginity to Ashley. His exact words, you ask? Ahem: “I lost my virginity to this girl named Ashley. You can probably just Google it.”

Joe and Blanda Eggenschwiler, a Swiss-born model and graphic designer, reportedly began dating in November 2012 after being introduced by a mutual friend and were together for almost two years. At the time, Joe called Blanda “someone I really care about” and said “we get each other.”

Sadly, no word on why they called it quits. A rep for Joe simply confirmed to Us Weekly that “Joe and Blanda have mutually decided to part ways” but “the split was amicable and the two remain friends, with great respect for one another.”

Joe dated yet another super-famous lady when he coupled up with Gigi Hadid in June 2015. At the time, Taylor Swift insisted it was totally fine that one of her BFFs was dating one of her exes, and Gigi even brought him to Taylor’s Fourth of July party. But after five months together, things seemed to end on a sour note.

The model ultimately began dating Zayn Malik two weeks after her split from Joe, of which he said publicly, “I think it’s interesting that she moved on so quickly. I mean it was definitely very quick.” Joe proceeded to unfollow Gigi on social media and delete a picture of himself with Zayn. Yikes.

And if that wasn’t enough, when Joe was asked to play “Shag, Marry, Kill” with his exes on Watch What Happens Live, Joe chose to marry Demi, shag Taylor, and kill Gigi. Okay!

Joe and his ex-wife Sophie Turner first sparked dating rumors in October 2016, and soon thereafter, the actor was spotted spending the holidays with the musician and his family. They kept their relationship pretty private at the beginning, but once they got engaged in October 2017, they started sharing their love with the public.

In July 2019, they tied the knot at Château du Martinet in Provence (that is, after a very campy Vegas ceremony a month prior), and went on to welcome two daughters together. Fans were then shocked when TMZ broke the news of their split in September 2023. Three days later, Joe and Sophie released a statement on Instagram, sharing that they’d “mutually decided to amicably end our marriage.”

It took a full year, but in September 2024, their marriage was officially dissolved.

Romance rumors between Joe and model Stormi Bree initially began swirling when they were spotted walking through a private airport terminal in Los Cabos, Mexico in January 2024. Soon after, the celebs continued to take their love ~international~ with trips to Aspen and even Australia ahead of the Jonas Brothers’ February shows in Sydney.

Fast-forward to March of that year, and a source told Us Weekly that “Joe is still dating Stormi and things are going really well. They haven’t used the love word yet, but they’ve been seeing each other for a few months now and have been getting more serious over the past several weeks.” Meanwhile, “He hasn’t introduced his kids to Stormi yet, but if things continue going well like they have been, then he would definitely be open to them meeting sometime soon.”

Sadly, by June 2024, these two were done, with multiple outlets reporting on the split. But the musician didn’t fly solo for long, because just two weeks after, he was snapped with...

Joe and Lebanese actor Laila Abdallah raised eyebrows when they were photographed looking very coupley on a beach in Athens, Greece. However, a source later told Us Weekly that the two were “just friends” and “were together at the One and Only opening in Greece” with a group that included Luke Evans and Liam Hemsworth.

Joe was first linked to Puerto Rican model Tatiana Gabriela in September 2025, with a source later telling People that they “first started to get to know each other late last summer.”

The two kept the cuteness going for just about a year before Us Weekly revealed in November 2026 that the couple had called it quits, with a source saying, “They had a really fun summer traveling together, but then things shifted in the relationship. Things started to shift as Joe got busier with work commitments and they were spending more time apart.”

#6728. 8 Pantry Items Pro Organizers Say You Should Toss ASAP

Your expired spices and stale snacks are taking up prime shelf space.

Delish editors handpick every product we feature. We may earn commission from the links on this page.

No one wants to waste perfectly good food—I’m more likely to eat something slightly past its expiration date than throw it out prematurely—but some pantry items are just taking up space.

Dusty canned foods, old spices, expired baking ingredients, and more past-their-prime goods serve little to no culinary value. Getting rid of them will actually help you identify the pantry items that you can still use before they go bad. But how can you keep your pantry organized more regularly, and what exactly should you throw away?

Go through every item in your pantry at least twice a year, recommends Nicole Gabai, professional organizer, founder of B. Organized, and author of The Art of Organizing: An Artful Guide to an Organized Life.

If a twice-yearly check-in feels too daunting, consider a weekly five-minute check-in to discard expired goods, says Shaniece Jones, professional organizer and founder of Closet Therapy. “Organize by accessibility and intention,” she says. “Keep your daily go-tos at eye level, heavy items like large cans of oil and bottled water on lower shelves, and seasonal or rarely used items up high.” Labels, Lazy Susans, and drawer dividers are some of her go-to tools for organizing the pantry.

As you start cleaning, check off the following items—these are some of the most common pantry items our organizing experts find past their prime.

While many shelf-stable foods, such as canned goods, are still safe to eat past their expiration date, you should still check for signs of spoilage. “You can tell canned goods have spoiled if they are rusted, or if you see the cans bulge or dent,” Gabai says.

Labeling pantry items helps you check how long they’ve been open. For opened packages and bulk goods you’ve decanted into containers, write the date on the label so you know when you opened them.

Spices that have lost their smell? Time to go. “If it smells flat or just not aromatic, then it needs to be replaced since it will add very little, if any, flavor to what you’re cooking,” Gabai says. She also notes that ground spices typically last one to two years, while dried herbs can last up to three years.

“Baking ingredients like flour, baking powder, and baking soda can lose their effectiveness over time,” says Meaghan Kessman, founder of Meaghan Kessman Home Organization.

Eventually, flour will go bad, but how long it takes to get rancid depends on how it’s stored and the type of flour. In general, Gabai says it will last at room temperature for six to eight months. She recommends storing it in an airtight container in the fridge or freezer.

“Check for expiration dates and any signs of spoilage, such as clumping or an off smell,” Kessman says. “If you’re unsure, test baking powder by adding a teaspoon to hot water—if it bubbles, it’s still good to use.”

We often buy cooking oils in bulk, but keep in mind that they eventually go bad. “Oils and fats, such as olive oil, vegetable oil, and shortening, can become rancid if stored for too long. Rancid oils have a sour or off smell,” Kessman says. “Always check the expiration date and store oils in a cool, dark place to keep them fresh longer.”

Gabai recommends storing oils in a cool, dark place and disposing of them properly; freeze in a zip-top bag, then toss in the trash (never pour down the drain).

These pantry staples are oil-rich, so they spoil faster than you might think. “They can last about 6 months at room temperature, but if you freeze them, they can last a year or more,” Gabai says.

“Snacks and cereals can go stale quickly once opened,” Kessman says. She recommends checking expiration dates and signs of staleness, like an unusual smell or texture.

According to Gabai, grains, rice, and beans can last about one to two years, but brown rice and other whole grains will not last as long because of their higher oil content.

“Condiments like ketchup, mustard, and salad dressings often get pushed to the back of the pantry and forgotten,” Kessman says. “Check for expiration dates and any signs of separation, discoloration, or off smells.”

Honey is the rare exception that probably isn't spoiled. The National Honey Board states that honey has a shelf life of approximately two years, although it can last much longer if stored correctly in a sealed container.

“Honey was found in the tombs of kings and royalty as far back as 3,000 years in Egypt—and it was still edible!” Gabai notes.

If you notice crystallization, Gabai recommends gently heating the honey container in a pot of water.

#6727. Sean Duffy Is a Perfect Reality-TV Politician. Too Bad for America.

As a fellow MTV veteran, I recognize that the transportation secretary has been practicing for this moment since his Real World days.

Yesterday, in the wake of Friday’s grounding of all flights in the continental United States and the weekend of cancellations and chaos that ensued, Secretary of Transportation Sean Duffy made the rounds of the Sunday-morning political talk shows to make this face.

It’s been a tough few days for Duffy, and Duffy has been huffy. From Friday’s press conference with Markwayne Mullin, to his own microphonically challenged solo outing on Saturday, through all five morning-show appearances, Duffy has checked all the boxes the modern Republican official must: look aggrieved, place blame elsewhere, praise the president, be white. It’s all television, after all, and there’s a role to be played.

Sean Duffy has been at this for decades. You know he was on the sixth season of The Real World, and you know he met his wife and fellow Real Worlder Rachel Campos when they participated in an early season of what would become The Real World/Road Rules Challenge. But when you really go back and look, you’ll see that they’ve both been practicing for this moment since the ’90s. Game recognizes game; as a fellow alumnus of 20th-century MTV, let me walk you through it.

The Real World was born when MTV decided to try its hand at creating a weekly soap opera, in the style of the era-defining Beverly Hills 90210 and Melrose Place. But cable budgets being what they are, a full scripted show was untenable, so producers had the bold idea to do away with actors and writers entirely. The first couple seasons of The Real World kept the focus on art by casting young artists: Julie the emerging dancer amid the model and writer and singer-songwriters in season 1, Jon the wannabe Garth Brooks in season 2. But that went out the window quick, as producers and aspiring castmates figured out the same thing at the same time: The job was to stir the pot. So by season 3, deeply unpleasant bike messenger Puck was dropped into the San Francisco house to clash with saintly AIDS educator Pedro, with whom conservative Rachel initially refused to share a bathroom for fear of sink-based HIV transmission. In 1994.

By season 6, casts seemed chosen specifically to play a predetermined part and drive each other up the wall. Real World: Boston features Stanford med student Kameelah and ladies man Syrus, Catholic Texan Elka and Manhattan-born mess on wheels Montana. And then there was Sean Duffy, Wisconsin boy, lumberjack-games participant, and future secretary of transportation. Sean occupied the Cute Boy spot in the cast that had been held by Aaron in Los Angeles and St. Louis race car driver Mike in London. But this being 1997, he was also a different cultural archetype: the knee-jerk Rush Limbaugh–era conservative. Accent on jerk. Here’s a moment when Sean makes fun of Elka’s underwear and then immediately denies it.

Just straight-up, reflexively, smoothly denying he did something we just saw him do, because he did it on camera and into a microphone. Lying with a straight face. This would become a job skill.

It’s a little like something we saw Sean’s future wife Rachel do on her season. In an early episode, she engages in the hot, safe rebellious act of 1994 by getting her navel pierced. In a later episode, she gets a telephone call from her angry mother, who has seen the belly-button bauble in a photograph (a physical photograph, sent through the U.S. Mail—again, we lived differently in the ’90s). Rachel assures her mother it’s not a piercing but instead a sticker she put there. Forget the fact that, again, all of this is on camera and the mom is going to learn the truth soon enough. Watching back then, I remember thinking two things: One, this person lies very easily, and two, I think she might be one of those people who’s conservative just so they don’t upset their parents.

Anyway. Sean and Rachel did The Challenge and got married and had three hundred and fifty children. Sean became a congressman for Wisconsin; Rachel tried out for The View but never quite nailed it and now she’s on Fox & Friends sometimes. And because it all comes back to reality television and personal branding, the Duffy family spent a few weeks of Sean’s tenure as secretary of transportation to film a show. The Great American Road Trip was supposed to have aired on Fox Nation, but instead it got dumped onto YouTube, where most episodes are hovering around 10k views.

Also, Duffy’s son-in-law Michael Alfonso, who’s 26, is set to become the youngest person in Congress, having won a seat in Wisconsin’s 7th District in this month’s elections. Alfonso’s campaign was buoyed by generous campaign donations from numerous transportation companies, executives, and lobbyists, including a $2,500 contribution from Air Space Intelligence’s PAC in March. In June, the FAA awarded the AI start-up a contract worth up to $875.95 million. The company’s SMART system went nationwide days before the November 13 ground stop, though the FAA and the company said the outage was unrelated to SMART.

I’m sure it’s nothing. But I’m even more sure Duffy will say it is.

The way that John F. Kennedy understood television in the way Richard Nixon did not, Sean Duffy understands reality television in a way few others do. He’s lived it. He understands it as a marketing exercise. Knowing your brand, hitting the beats of the scene you’re supposed to play, showing up with a yearbook picture-day haircut and lying with a smile will get you far in this world. Whether he survives this turbulence or not, he’s learned that lesson.

Sean and Rachel have been at this for decades, but as viewers so have we. We can see them as they are: dishonest children, playing a role, begging for approval, just trying not to get yelled at.

The White House to Rep. Robert Garcia: “WAH!”

#6725. ‘He Needs To Be Layered’: Bond Girl Léa Seydoux Shares the Most Important Traits for the Next 007

What it takes to play James Bond, from the actor who’s played his most influential love interest.

If there’s one Bond girl who has influenced the course of 007 history more than anyone, it’s Dr. Madeleine Swann. Played by French actor Léa Seydoux, who is this month’s ELLE UK cover star, psychotherapist Dr. Swann disrupts the James Bond narrative in a way that no previous lover has done: She challenges his misogyny.

Appearing in the last two films in the series—Spectre (2015) and No Time to Die (2021)—Dr. Swann has a long-term relationship with secret agent 007, eventually telling him that he’s the father of her daughter, making Seydoux’s part in the legendary franchise unique.

Asked whether she would consider a return to the franchise, now under the creative direction of Amazon MGM Studios and without Daniel Craig as 007, the actor said: “I really loved working with Barbara Broccoli and her brother Michael [G. Wilson]. It was one of the best experiences, a pure delight. And I don’t know, it will be different.”

She has already worked with Denis Villeneuve, the director attached to the next Bond film, in Dune: Part Two. “He’s a great choice, let’s see. I’m curious how they will reinvent it. But for me, it will never be the same because it was Barbara’s father who initiated the Bond movies. Now, I don’t know,” she told ELLE. “It became American….I hope they will keep the British spirit.”

As for casting Seydoux in the role, former Bond producer Barbara Broccoli said that her scenes in No Time to Die still move her to tears. “When we were filming the goodbye scene at the Italian train station, Léa approached it with such subtlety—she grasped her stomach to express her pain, but also the secret heartbreak that she was carrying James’s child. It moved me to tears then and continues to move me each time I watch it.”

As for the question of who will play Bond, she won’t be drawn into the speculation. (This was before Robert Pattinson was announced to take on the mantle on November 16.) Seydoux admits that finding the right person to replace Daniel Craig is difficult. “He needs to be layered, you know? And that is something that’s hard to find in young actors.”

Léa Seydoux appears next in Gentle Monster, which begins streaming on Netflix on November 18.

#6723. Our 10 Favorite Men’s Fisherman Sweaters to Wear All Winter

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The coziest, most rugged layers a man could ever buy.

We love fisherman sweaters for the same reason we love a great martini: They’re classic, and they’re comforting. A proper fisherman knit brings the whole winter wardrobe into alignment. The chunky texture instantly upgrades jeans and boots, warmth doesn’t sacrifice style, and that easy, rugged-prep vibe that works at the office as well as it does on the couch. In other words, it’s the rare cold-weather staple that’s as practical as it is handsome.

Now, it’s crucial to remember that when it comes to the backstory of most garments, there tends to be a lot of lore mixed in with the genuine history. That said, here’s how the story generally goes. Traditional fisherman sweaters hail from Ireland, where in the late 1800s to the mid-1900s, they were popular for men who had to work long hours on fishing boats. Usually, fisherman sweaters were hand-knit by their wives or by local artisans, and they were always designed to be sturdy and warm, keeping the wind and chill out. Cable-knit patterns became popular for fisherman sweaters. You might know these sweaters as Aran sweaters, referencing the Aran Islands, where the textured design has become a specialty.

In 2026, you probably don’t need a fisherman sweater to keep you warm while you’re on the water off Ireland’s west coast. (Or maybe you do! We don’t know your story.) However, you do need a fisherman sweater for the sheer coziness and looks they provide, though. Our favorite options of all time are below, tested and reviewed by Esquire editors (with some photographed on Esquire’s editor in chief, Michael Sebastian). Anyway, these fisherman sweaters are seriously making us want to take some fishing lessons. Anyone know where we can do that?

You don’t need to break the bank for a high-quality fisherman sweater that’ll last you years to come. Quince is proof. Crafted from organic cotton, this thick, warm sweater is a perfect layering piece for winter—put a tee or a button-down underneath, a topcoat or a puffer on top, and you’re golden. The rib detailing offers some extra texture and style, and relaxed raglan sleeves offer more freedom of movement.

Ok, so the pictured sweater is no longer in stock. The cable knit rollneck was seasonal, but there is always a fisherman-y option in the collection. Honestly, whichever J. Crew rollneck hits this bar at any point in time, that’s likely my favorite fisherman sweater of the moment.

L.L. Bean crafts a classic fisherman sweater to a tee. It has everything you’d want in the style: a thick, heavyweight fabric that carries some structure with it; a cable-knit design; and thick cotton yarn that keeps the chill out and the warmth in. This style is a go-to for something timeless without too much fuss or frill. It has a slim fit, so if you want something baggier, more classically cut, make sure to size up.

Okay, hear me out—this isn’t your traditional fisherman sweater, but you can make a damn good excuse for Polo Ralph Lauren’s ultra-soft, ultra-luxe cable-knit sweater when you feel how unbelievably plush this is. This is the fisherman sweater you go to when you want something distinctly elevated and chic but you still want to have a cable-knit design and a fit you can dress down with joggers and slides.

You may already know that we at Esquire have an affinity for shawl collar cardigans. With their laid-back Western vibes and classic Americana silhouette, shawl collar cardigans look handsome on just about any guy. One of our favorites is this fisherman style from Filson. It’s made from thick, warm cotton and features a classic button front and ribbing on the hem and cuffs.

If you, like commerce editor Luke Guillory, aren’t into the super chunky fisherman sweaters and are looking for something a little more lightweight that you can easily layer, this Buck Mason pick should be right up your alley. Luke has even endorsed the style, lauding the sweater for its warm yet lightweight feel, perfect not-too-boxy but not-too-slim cut, and durable cotton that can be machine washed and still not miss a beat.

This gorgeous wool fisherman sweater is from Finisterre, a surf clothing brand from Cornwall, England, so it’s specifically made for cold, windy days on the water. It’s made from 100 percent heavyweight, pure new wool with a natural crimp, which means it creates natural pockets of air to help insulate you (even if you’re not on the water). One writer says it’s one of his go-to winter sweaters.

Brooks Brothers’ woolly sweater is so damn good I might just have to take up the art of fishing to pay proper tribute to it. With a soft and cozy yet breathable wool fabric and cable-knit pattern, this relaxed style puts a contemporary spin on the traditional fisherman sweater. It’s warm, soft, and stylish, and it looks just as good with dress pants and loafers as it does with jeans and sneakers.

Begg x Co’s Isla cashmere sweater is another fantastic, high-quality cashmere option. It’s just oppressively beautiful. Begg x Co operates its own mills in Scotland, which shows up in the world-class materials and construction, but the vibe isn’t overly capital-F-Fashion the way some French or Italian luxury brands can feel. It’s timeless, understated luxury. Made from 8‑ply cashmere, it lands in a perfect mid-weight sweet spot for fall and winter: substantial and warm without being bulky. It’s definitely quite pricey, but you’re paying for the highest level of quality and finish.

This sweater from Ghiaia Cashmere is divine, surprising considering the brand is based out of southern California. The mock neck is the star here—cozy without the bulk of a turtleneck—and the rich brown color is lovely (though it also comes in a clean cream if you want something lighter). A chest pocket adds a surprisingly practical touch, making it feel more functional than you’d ever expect a sweater to be.

#6718. I Have Serious Questions About the 2026 Grammy Nominations

Is the Recording Academy out of its mind?

The Grammys, more than any other awards-voting body of the big EGOT four, is easily the most chaotic. Sure, we can shake our fists in the air all day about how Stanley Kubrick somehow never won an Oscar, or ask ourselves why the Tony Awards ever allowed themselves to become a place where SpongeBob SquarePants: The Broadway Musical leads with 12 nominees. But recalling Grammy events like Macklemore beating Kendrick Lamar, Esperanza Spalding claiming Best New Artist over Justin Bieber and Drake, and Beck taking home Album of the Year instead of Beyoncé, it’s a wonder just what happens between when the votes are submitted and when the winners are revealed.

This year? The Recording Academy announced on Monday, November 16, that Kanye West is a Grammy nominee once again. And there’s a single category where Bruce Springsteen, Phoebe Bridgers, and Charli XCX are lumped together, so I guess they’re considered basically the same kind of artist to voters. Plus, new ideas like “Best Asian Pop Music Performance” are moving forward despite immediately losing support from the most prominent artist in that category, and Best New Artist nominees can apparently put out their fifth studio album and still be considered. What’s going on?

So, before the Grammys decide to ignore all of our desires and complaints and just shower Taylor Swift with more little golden phonographs on February 7, 2027, let’s try to make sense of the 2026 Grammy nominees below.

The first thought that entered my mind when I saw this year’s nominees for Album of the Year was, Are we confirming who will be available to perform before announcing the nominees now? Because even if so, I think Bruno Mars would perform either way.

It’s very cool to see how women dominated the music field this year, especially because they truly did. Olivia Rodrigo, Phoebe Bridgers, and Rosalía all earning Album of the Year nominations is a no-brainer. Olivia Dean, who won Best New Artist last year, and Ella Langley, who wrote “Choosin’ Texas,” make up the rest of the list. (Did we really love that entire nineteen-track Langley album?) Plus, of course, the obligatory Taylor Swift nominations.

But were Bruno Mars and Leon Thomas—who are tied with seven nominations each after the leading women, including an Album of the Year nod for Mars—the two men with the best offerings this year?

Bruno Mars had a very good song on The Romantic with “Why You Wanna Fight?” The Luther Vandross-esque R&B ballad was everything you’d look for in a Song of the Year nominee. But instead, it looks like Bruno is going all in on “Risk It All” and “I Just Might,” the latter of which is more of a “Happy” by Pharrell Williams-type song that somehow isn’t the lead single of the next Trolls movie.

Sorry Madonna! Apologies Robyn, Tinashe, Slayyter, and every other pop artist who soundtracked our year on the dance floor with some of the best albums of 2026. Breaking into the top four major categories still looks like a no-go.

Instead, Madonna, Robyn, and Tinashe were relegated to categories such as Best Dance Pop Recording and Best Dance/Electronic Album, even despite critical acclaim. If you want a Record of the Year nod, try writing a song for Toy Story 5.

Don Toliver is up for Best New Artist. The Houston rapper, who was signed to Travis Scott’s label in 2018, released his fifth studio album, Octane, this year. He had a song on the F9 soundtrack in 2021—which isn’t even the latest Fast & Furious movie—and his 2023 album, Love Sick, featured James Blake, Future, and Justin Bieber. But here we are, in 2026, watching him compete for Best New Artist.

Equally puzzling: Remi Wolf is nominated for Best New Artist. The singer’s exceptional debut record, Juno, came out in 2021. And her best record to date, Mud, was released recently on October 9, just outside of the Grammy submission window that closed in late August. But following the logic of how Olivia Dean won Best New Artist last year even though Dean’s album is competing this year, it’s clear that the whole Best New Artist category needs some rethinking.

2026 wasn’t a banner year for rap. I can admit that. Sure, we had very good records from A$AP Rocky, Denzel Curry, Vince Staples, JPEGMAFIA, Isaiah Rashad, Genesis Owusu, and Baby Keem. But none of the musicians I listed above went recognized this year, except for Keem’s single “Best Rap Performance” nomination.

Instead, the Recording Academy nominated Wale’s Everything Is a Lot—the Washington, D.C., rapper’s best and most mature album of the last decade, but far less influential than Rocky’s or Keem’s releases. And if not for Drake’s long-running boycott, the absence of Iceman’s “Janice STFU” just looks silly. But if you want to talk ridiculous, let’s see how Rap Album of the Year nominee Kanye West plays out for the Recording Academy over the next couple of months.

Harry Styles won Album of the Year for Harry’s House in 2023. He beat out Un Verano Sin Ti by Bad Bunny, Renaissance by Beyoncé, Mr. Morale & the Big Steppers by Kendrick Lamar, and 30 by Adele. It was a major upset. And just last month, the former One Direction member wrapped a historic thirty-night residency at Madison Square Garden in New York City in support of his latest album, Kiss All the Time. Disco, Occasionally.

Now, I’m not saying Kiss All the Time is a masterwork, but it’s strange that Styles didn’t receive a single nomination for his Harry’s House follow-up. “Aperture” was very catchy! Taylor Swift can’t sneeze without the Recording Academy inventing a new category for her to compete in. But an artist who just won Album of the Year can find himself completely shut out, even if his latest record is a success?

The global K-pop megastars reunited this year following mandatory military service in South Korea for one of the genre’s most-anticipated albums of all time, ARIRANG. The record paid homage to the popular Korean folk song of the same name while also pushing the K-pop genre forward with hip-hop-heavy material. ARIRANG’s fourteen tracks also filled the top fourteen spots of Spotify’s global Top 50 chart on its first day of release on March 20. I’ll stop there, but there are a hundred more ways to say that it was one of the biggest albums of the year.

But when the Recording Academy announced a new category this year, “Best Asian Pop Music Performance,” the group made the decision not to submit for the Grammys this year. The move was widely regarded as a protest of the new category, which many interpreted as a ghettoization to keep the rise of popular Asian acts away from the general field categories. “I hope that music can be heard and loved for what it is, rather than being divided by region or language,” the group posted on social media.

On Monday, November 16, the Academy went through with the category anyway, announcing nominees including Lisa from Blackpink, the Japanese pop-metal group Babymetal, the K-pop group IVE, and the Filipino girl group Bini, among others. But with BTS joining Drake in his boycott of the Grammys, I wonder how the Recording Academy will fare without two of the world’s most popular acts entertaining its awards show.

The Great Divide, a seventeen-track rock album that turned Kahan into one of the most popular touring musicians of 2026, was considered a favorite to receive a nomination in every major category this year. Songs like “American Cars” dealt with past failures and road trip blues, while lead single “The Great Divide” tackled how fame added extra struggles to many of his already-strained relationships. And Kahan had the resume to become a major category contender this year. He had a Number One record! He did the full press tour, he performed on Saturday Night Live, and he sold out stadiums all across the country.

But it must have been an omen when Kahan sang that “California’s so much more than some awards show” on the deluxe edition’s “Orbiter,” because he received just one nomination on Monday for Best Contemporary Folk Album. It’s a shame.

Best Album Cover is back. The category returned last year and accurately awarded Tyler, the Creator’s zany Chromakopia artwork, which featured the artist wearing a black, ceramic mask. He told Complex at the time that it symbolized how the new music was “so honest, that I think I had to wear a mask of my own face to get some of that sh*t out.”

But what about this year’s nominees? Well, Mavis Staples’s Sad and Beautiful World artwork depicts the artist sitting at a table. Interpol’s This Mirror Weighs a Ton artwork is a chandelier made of security cameras, and Lily Allen’s West End Girl is a painting of the artist wearing a polka-dot jacket. Are these nominees... the best album covers of the year?

Charli XCX’s photograph of Martin Scorsese, John Cale, and Marc Jacobs was easily the much-talked-about album cover of the year, even though Music, Fashion, Film wasn’t nominated. Olivia Rodrigo’s upside-down swinging for you seem pretty sad for a girl so in love is definitely one of the more memorable covers that come to mind. Same goes for Robyn’s blurry and nude Sexistential photo, the politics behind Kneecap’s Fenian, Thundercat biting his dark leather jacket on the cover of Distracted, or even a pregnant Latto posing with a baby tiger for the Big Mama cover ahead of her cheetah-themed baby shower.

We might not have a specific shade of green to color this year and make Best Album Cover an easy choice, but if you’re going to bring the category back? At least wow us.

Skip the dried-out chunks of bread this year and reach for some frozen waffles. Trust me when I say this will be your new favorite form of stuffing. #6711. Cranberry Cream Cheese Spread

Of all the incredible dips we’ve created over the years, our cranberry whipped feta dip is an internet and personal favorite—so much so that I just had to come back with an equally luxurious variation: this cranberry-topped whipped cream cheese spread. With the holiday party season just around the corner, this spread is begging to be included in your party spread. Plus, it's as easy to make as it is delicious—a tangy, sweet cranberry sauce gets finished with brandy, then is spooned atop a lemony whipped cream cheese for a guaranteed winning combo.

Fresh Or Frozen Cranberries? I used both while testing, and they both work interchangeably. I found the fresh cranberries broke down a little more than frozen, so it depends on whether or not you’re looking for more or less texture in your sauce.

What temperature should the cream cheese be? Make sure the cream cheese is chilled and softened but not super cold. It should be somewhat pliable when whipping to avoid lumps; this will ensure the mixture is light and airy. A little heavy cream will loosen the mixture if it's too thick.

The lemon zest: I found that the cream cheese layer needed a little more oomph. If you get a bite of cream cheese on a cracker, it shouldn’t be one-dimensional. Adding a touch of lemon zest makes it more palatable.

Storage:Store the cream cheese spread, with or without cranberry sauce, in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 5 days.

#6708. Is KATSEYE Following the Rules of K-Pop?

Fans are worried by Megan’s decision to perform in the face of a concussion, but maybe the debate is about more than one group, but about an entire industry (or two).

Online debates about celebrity work culture sparked when KATSEYE’s Megan Skindiel, who is recovering from a concussion, recently performed seated and in sunglasses during a show in Austin on the group’s WILDWORLD Tour. The next night in Dallas, video surfaced of the artist taking part in the group’s intense dance choreography, while wearing what many speculate to be a heart monitor.

Fans expressed grave concern for her well-being, questioning whether she should be performing at all. While it is important to note that we don’t actually know whether it was Megan’s own decision to perform, many still accused KATSEYE’s label, HYBE x Geffen Records, of potentially overworking their artists at the expense of their health. The label is a partnership between the South Korean entertainment company HYBE (home to groups like BTS, Illit, TXT, and Seventeen) and the American label Geffen Records (home to artists such as Olivia Rodrigo, Selena Gomez, Lady Gaga, and Lana Del Rey).

And there’s another relevant context: With Manon and Sophia already on hiatus, Megan’s absence would leave the group with just three members, only half their original formation of six, and for that reason the group had already postponed three shows. As of now, neither the label nor the group has publicly addressed the criticism.

For longtime K-pop fans, none of this is entirely shocking. Those who follow the industry have most likely already witnessed their idols push through panic attacks, fainting spells, extreme sleep deprivation, injuries from falls, and many other mental and physical struggles in order to give their fans the concert experience they were promised. But for many Western fans, this may be the first look at the grueling expectations that the K-pop world has for its artists, which helps explain the intense concern in their reactions.

KATSEYE is an especially interesting case for us to explore this friction. The group was formed through Dream Academy, a survival competition run by HYBE and Geffen Records, where the finalists trained mainly in Los Angeles, with trips to HYBE’s headquarters in Seoul. While KATSEYE’s music is mostly in English, the group promotes in both the U.S. and South Korea, exemplifying the group’s unique positioning between the two music industries, each with its own set of rules and ideas of what a pop career demands.

Commercially, that meeting point keeps getting closer, with the dramatic rise of K-pop’s popularity and its growing presence in mainstream Western media. And that cross-cultural exchange is reflected in the music itself: English words increasingly make up the majority of the lyrics in K-pop songs, and many labels are now intentionally debuting members who are proficient in English.

It also helps to compare how music careers get built, starting with how the artists are even first discovered by the world. Many of the West’s biggest stars began as those who were organically noticed: Justin Bieber was discovered through cover videos on YouTube, and Taylor Swift was spotted as a teenager playing Nashville’s Bluebird Cafe. K-pop runs on a completely different model: one where the company trains the individual into being an artist through a strict, meticulous formula within a larger system of strategic control.

Those who make it through the company’s insanely competitive rounds of auditions will then spend years as trainees with no guarantee of debuting. Those years typically involve regular evaluations, private lessons, as well as sometimes group living for management and surveillance purposes. Trainees often agree to be prescribed diet plans and sometimes even cosmetic surgery, as well as close monitoring of their private lives including harsh restrictions on dating and phone use to ensure that their status as trainees remains a secret. And what may be even more jarring for those new to this culture is that many of these trainees are often as young as 13, with more and more labels debuting their idols before they are even close to being legal adults.

In exchange comes a carefully engineered shot at success, with executives and experts calibrating the exact combination of members, visual concept, songs, and more to optimize audience appeal. Audiences got a glimpse of this process during Dream Academy, though in a more Westernized version. Whatever ethical questions this process raises, it works, at least commercially. All the biggest K-pop groups have come out of this exact system, and the labels continue to stay closely involved throughout their careers.

But the label isn’t the only relationship that heavily shapes a K-pop artist’s life. There’s also the one with the fans. Western audiences are no strangers to fan culture, from Beatlemania to Swifties, and plenty of domestic K-pop fandoms have a U.S. counterpart. However, one notable cultural difference is one of degree. Take whatever you’ve seen from American fandoms and multiply it by a hundred, and you start to approach the level of devotion an average K-pop stan extends to their bias, or favorite member. They follow idols to concert after concert and every public appearance in between, and they also work together, using step-by-step guides to stream songs in ways that send them up the charts, sometimes even outperforming many mainstream Western artists on global charts like the Billboard Hot 100.

And that kind of devotion comes with expectations. Many groups have dedicated fan-chatting apps, such as HYBE’s Weverse and the subscription-based Bubble, where artists can post and chat with fans almost directly, and fans come to expect regular contact through this service. Idols are also expected to keep up a cheerful image, to avoid canceling a show that fans paid for at all costs, and to immediately apologize when they fall short, even when the reason is an injury.

Public dating, meanwhile, has long been treated as a career-threatening betrayal for these artists. In 2024, after aespa’s Karina was revealed to be dating an actor, she faced a wave of backlash, causing her to post a handwritten apology to fans. Norms have loosened for established stars, but newcomers, or what the industry calls shin-ins, are discouraged, or often barred from it, by both their companies and their fans.

Because they’re still largely construed as Western artists, KATSEYE is, to a large degree, exempt from these rules. For example, members have been comparatively open about their dating lives—a luxury a Korea-based group would most likely not be afforded. But KATSEYE is still managed by a K-pop company, trained by a K-pop system. It wouldn’t be surprising that some of that ethos of always putting the fans first is internalized within the group’s own decision-making, shaping how much they feel they owe their fans in ways a Western artist might not.

From a Western perspective, it makes sense why all of this might feel like an oppressive system that needs reform, or even dismantling. And the criticism exists internally as well: On November 16, South Korea’s Culture Ministry officially announced the launch of a comprehensive survey to review concerns over young performers’ working conditions following the NewJeans controversy. However, one could argue that Western criticism can often be translated by domestic fans as a kind of white-savior complex, with “outsiders” interfering in the methods and systems that have existed without their attention for decades, while deciding it must assimilate to their terms and standards. Especially when many of these critics lack the necessary understanding of the complicated nuances of how K-pop’s cultural specificity largely reflects South Korea’s distinct historical, political, and socioeconomic landscape—one that dramatically differs from the U.S.

These aspects of K-pop are deeply normalized within the industry itself: Trainee systems are shown openly in documentaries and TV series, including ones the companies themselves help produce, and artists often talk about those years candidly, suggesting that many of them may not see these practices as the problem outsiders do. And maybe there is also a truth in the idea that K-pop’s impressive commercial success is partly a product of the very controls critics object to, including the symbiotic relationships between artists and their labels, and between artists and their fans. Without that culture, we would probably not have what we now know to be BLACKPINK or BTS.

None of this makes the overworking of artists acceptable, nor does it erase much of the valid concern so many people feel. We are allowed to worry when our favorite artist seems to be pushed too far, whether it’s by their own drive, their label’s instructions, or by the weight of fans’ expectations. And we’re allowed to question the very systems that make that possible—in K-pop, in Hollywood, or anywhere else.

But it’s also worth asking what it might mean for a system with decades of independent history—one that grew largely outside Western norms—to navigate a global spotlight (and the equally globalized standards that come with it). Where does cultural context end and basic safety begin? And who gets to decide what “better” looks like, if not the artists living it?

#6707. The One Record in Sports That Will Never Be Broken

In an age when numbers are paramount, a single statistic from hockey stands taller than the rest.

Back in my full-time sportswriting days—and, in fact, throughout my journalism career—I managed to disguise my fundamental innumeracy. Then suddenly, quite recently, numbers became paramount in sports. Baseball led the way, as it does in so many areas where boredom is involved. Now, thanks to gambling’s explosion in popularity, statistics have become one of the primary ways to be a fan.

In this era of analytics and the tyranny of numbers, there is one statistic that remains not only my favorite but that is likely never to fall, and it comes from, of all sports, hockey. It is this, which boggles the mind: Wayne Gretzky ended his career in 1999 with a total of 2,857 points, which includes goals and assists. Factor out the goals, and he’s still at 1,963, which is 42 more points than the player with the next-highest total, Jaromir Jagr, who is retired.

The first time I ever saw Gretzky play was during the 1988 Stanley Cup playoffs, when my Boston Bruins faced his Edmonton Oilers. These were the dynastic Oilers, loaded with talent: Mark Messier. Jari Kurri. Glenn Anderson. Grant Fuhr between the pipes. All of them Hall of Famers. Amid all this clamorous talent, Gretzky was a phantom. He had this strange and wonderful ability to vanish in the middle of the action and then, suddenly, appear in exactly the right spot at exactly the right time to make exactly the right play.

Gretzky will likely be the leading point scorer in NHL history for the next several decades. In fact, you have to go all the way down to seventh place on the list to find an active player, Sidney Crosby, who is 202 short of Gretzky’s 1,963 assist-only number. Another active player, Alex Ovechkin, is in tenth place, but he’s got one skate out the door already. The next active players on the list are Evgeni Malkin and Patrick Kane, at 23rd and 24th.

Like Gretzky himself, this statistic disappears among all the more important numbers until it suddenly pops out to amaze and astound. I like Sidney Crosby a great deal as a player, but I hope he never gets there.

Charles P Pierce is the author of four books, most recently Idiot America, and has been a working journalist since 1976.

#6704. All the Times Robert Pattinson Basically Manifested Becoming the Next James Bond

Robert Pattinson has officially received his license to kill. As James Bond, that is. Hollywood’s favorite leading man was announced as the next 007 on Monday, November 16, after Puck News reported that he screen-tested for the role in the eleventh hour.

“James Bond is one of the most legendary characters in cinema, and to be entrusted with 007 is the honor of a lifetime,” Robert wrote in a statement, per ABC News. “To say this role is a dream come true is an understatement. I couldn’t be more excited about this partnership and the work ahead with this fantastic team.”

As it turns out, Robert has unknowingly manifested the role of a lifetime throughout his banger-after-banger career. No, seriously—R-Patz locked into Ariana Grande as Galinda-levels of manifesting here, whether he believes it or not.

Even after all these years as a Twihard, I’m genuinely shocked at how much this man was asked about potentially playing the famous spy. So, for the sake of your scrolling time, I’ve rounded up all the times Robert Pattinson manifested his role as James Bond, right here.

All roads lead back to Twilight. Robert got his big break playing swoonworthy sparkly vampire Edward Cullen in the film franchise, and the first movie lowkey predicted his current role as 007. Don’t believe me? Check out this scene.

When Bella’s friend group discusses the school’s upcoming Monte Carlo prom theme, Eric says, “Gambling, tuxedos, and Bond, James Bond,” before the camera cuts to Edward and Bella stepping out of his car.

Robert starred alongside another Bond, Pierce Brosnan, in 2010’s Remember Me. While doing press for the movie, he was constantly asked whether he’d want to take on the iconic role at some point.

During an interview with Fandango, he admitted that he would “quite like to play a villain in a Bond film.”

“That would be fun,” he continued. “Or maybe a male, like, Bond girl. A Bond boy.”

The BBC separately asked if he’d be into the role, to which he sheepishly responded, “Oh, I don’t know. That’s quite big shoes to fill. If I did it right now, it’d probably be pretty bad. Pretty embarrassing.”

He remained humble at the time, but fast forward 16 years, and he’s officially been cast as 007 himself!

The Bond questions kept coming after Remember Me, though Daniel Craig had led the franchise since 2005.

During an interview with Shortlist, Rob said he was open to taking on the role in the future.

“Yeah definitely, but more like 20 years,” he said. “I think there’d be nothing worse than, like, ‘Let’s get a fresh-faced Bond!’ That would be the worst idea in the world. After Daniel Craig, you have to have some baggage. It would be ridiculous to reinvent [him] as some young posh kid. Or, if [he] was, I’d have to get so tortured in the first few scenes. I’d have to do the first film with one arm or something.”

In a separate conversation with the BBC, Rob was asked about the role again, responding, “Why do you keep asking me this? There already is a James Bond. I don’t know. I mean, I love James Bond. So, I don’t know.”

Okay, so this technically wasn’t spoken prophecy from R-Patz, but someone previously tied to another Bond project expressed interest in seeing the actor take on the famous role.

Danny Boyle, who was originally tapped to direct the last Bond movie, No Time to Die, told The Guardian that while he watched Robert’s flick High Life, “I was sitting there thinking: ‘Oh my God, they should get him to be the next Bond.’”
