#6665. How to Talk to Your Friends and Family About the Trans Community, According to Experts

If you get flustered whenever you speak up about trans rights, you're not alone. That's why we put together this list of expert-approved talking points.

Since Donald Trump took his second term in office, he and his administration have issued a series of discriminatory executive orders restricting access to gender-affirming care for trans youth, banning trans women and girls from participating in women’s sports, declaring that the federal government will only recognize two genders (impacting identifying documents like passports and visas), banning transgender people from serving in the military, and so much more.

Thankfully, many of these executive orders are being challenged in court. But the messaging has already infiltrated the brains of Trump supporters (and beyond) far and wide, normalizing these hateful thoughts and actions in our country.

So what do we do? We speak out and spread facts. Especially with those closest to us who might not be informed or might not understand. That’s not always easy though, especially when emotions get involved and you end up getting upset and flustered. To help, I tapped three experts to create a list of responses to common (and problematic) refrains about the trans community you might come across in future discussions.

In any discourse with someone who doesn’t think like you, it’s always important to make sure the other party knows you’re actually also listening—not just arguing. But if tensions do get high, the Q&As below can serve as a script of sorts to fall back on when you want to make sure you’re hitting all the key talking points while supporting and speaking up for the trans community.

Dr. Meredith McNamara: Actually, some people are born with neither or with genitalia that doesn’t fit binary concepts of sex. What sex is someone who is born without a vagina but has XX chromosomes (MRKH Syndrome)? What sex is someone who has XXY chromosomes and little to no detectable testosterone levels in adulthood (Klinefelter syndrome)?

But let’s take a step back and look at this from a different angle: Gender and sex are different. Gender identity develops long before the physical change of puberty. People generally know their gender long before secondary sex characteristics develop, because gender identity has its own strong biological basis rooted in the brain and it exists separately from chromosomes, gonads, and hormones.

Gillian Branstetter: And why not? Every culture in human history has had people who reject their gender assignment in one way or another, and decades of feminist legal progress has challenged the idea that biology must be our destiny. Transgender people come from all walks of life—every ethnicity, every background, every country, every political party. All people deserve the freedom to be themselves without fear, and that includes transgender people.

Shane Diamond: Everybody has a gender: an internal sense of self as it relates to the gendered world around us. For many people, that inner sense of self matches the sex they were assigned at birth—for example, someone assigned female at birth who knows herself to be a woman. This simply means they are cisgender! For other people, their inner sense of self is different from their sex assigned at birth, causing an incongruence with who they know themselves to be and how the world sees them. This person might call themselves transgender. That doesn’t make anyone’s gender more or less valid than someone else’s—it just means they’re different. At the end of the day, we don’t expect everyone to know exactly what it means to be transgender, but we do expect people to respect each other, even when we are different.

Gillian Branstetter: Transgender people face discrimination, mistreatment, and abuse across our lives. We’re four times as likely to experience violence and much more likely to experience poverty and homelessness. While nobody should have to pay such a price just for being true to themselves, I hardly think anyone wants that kind of “attention.” Also, transgender people are raised around cisgender people all the time—it never seems to “rub off” on us.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: In my practice, I only see the opposite—most young people who are trans try to present as cis for a period of time to deflect attention or to not stand out. It’s never been harder to be trans. While many things can be socially influenced, we know that gender identity cannot. Studies have shown that trans people report a long-standing knowledge of their gender identity—it often takes years before they disclose that identity outwardly. Gender conformity is the real social contagion at play, not trans identity.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Some people regret all types of medical care, whether it’s knee replacement surgery, spine surgery, or Accutane. With surgery and hormones, regret rates are less than 1 percent—among the lowest in all of medicine. But it’s also important to think about regret itself and what it means. People who express regret about surgery or hormones often say that it’s just too hard to be trans in an openly hostile society. For those people, it’s not health care at the root of regret—it’s housing discrimination, job loss, violence, and political targeting. Until we ensure the same dignities for trans people as we do for everyone else, we need to think about regret in dimensions and never use it as a reason to snuff out opportunities for care.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: You’d probably hold a door open for someone or make an effort to spell their name correctly in an email. You’d probably also expect others to get your pronouns right. Moreover, there are plenty of times when we simply can’t discern someone’s gender because they haven’t told us and we’ve never met them. We naturally revert to using gender-neutral pronouns in those cases. Keep that in mind and remember that something small can make someone else’s day.

Also, it’s totally normal to be nervous about getting this wrong. I get people’s pronouns wrong and it doesn’t feel great, but I own it and move on. I’ll usually say, “Hey, I got your pronouns wrong—next time, I’ll get it right!” It removes the awkwardness where the other person feels like they have to comfort the one making a mistake and it gets us back on track to whatever it was we were talking about.

Shane Diamond: I totally get it. It can feel unfamiliar to refer to someone using they/them pronouns, but we actually use those pronouns all the time for individuals. For example, if I found a backpack and was bringing it to a lost and found, I’d say, “Someone forgot their backpack.” I don’t know who forgot it, so using “their” acts as a singular person pronoun regardless of gender. It can take practice, but it’s not grammatically incorrect or even uncommon in our language. If using someone’s correct pronouns feels too clunky or too complicated, you can always skip pronouns and just use their name. But at the end of the day, using someone’s correct pronouns isn’t about you—it’s a way of showing respect for someone else.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Let’s get back to biology for a second: Ovaries and testicles both produce estrogen and testosterone. So this statement has some fundamental flaws. But let’s also think about what would happen if we applied this statement’s logic broadly: We’d cosign a lot of bad health care. We wouldn’t offer testosterone replacement therapy to cis men with low libido, we wouldn’t help ease cis women into menopause safely or respectfully, and we wouldn’t support people who lose those gonadal functions due to cancer. We wouldn’t be able to suppress life-threatening menstrual bleeding in people with bleeding disorders like hemophilia! I don’t think anyone wants that. If there’s a seat at the table of good health care for these cis people, then there’s a seat at the table for trans people, too.

Gillian Branstetter: The treatments transgender people access are no different from the treatments millions of cis people rely on for their health needs. And importantly, decisions about medical care should be kept between patients and their doctors—not politicians.

Gillian Branstetter: The good news is you don’t have to understand everything about a transgender person to know they’re as deserving of respect and equal treatment as everyone else, and that’s all most are asking for. Every day we interact with people of different races, religions, and backgrounds, and we don’t need to be an expert in their experience to trust them with their experience and treat them with the same dignity we would want for ourselves.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Imagine if you—as a woman—sprouted hair on your chest and had genitalia hanging outside of your body that made you feel completely unlike yourself. What if society would only treat you with dignity and respect if you wore masculine clothing? If you can step into that headspace for a second and experience a smidge of that discomfort, imagine feeling that every moment of your life. The fact that trans people can and do navigate gender dysphoria is beyond admirable. Let’s also reframe the concept of choice: People don’t want to be a gender—they are a gender. They choose whether to present themselves to society as that gender, and that choice is what gender-affirming care can facilitate.

Shane Diamond: We already have existing state and federal laws that protect people, specifically women, from predators, harassment, and assault. It’s already illegal to assault someone, regardless of gender or location. Despite fearmongering from anti-transgender voices, the truth is, transgender people are actually most at risk for being attacked in the restroom. Being that trans women are women and trans men are men, each should use the respective restroom that aligns with their gender. Ultimately, we’re all trying to do the same thing in restrooms—use a likely-under-serviced toilet and check our social media—regardless of our gender.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: Wow, this is news to me! Every patient I’ve had who’s gotten any form of gender-affirming surgery has gone through years of deep introspection about what’s right for them. Once they’ve arrived there, they’ve jumped through hoop after hoop after hoop to connect with the right providers.

Shane Diamond: First, let’s talk about what this really is: medically necessary healthcare for trans and nonbinary people. (“Gender-reassignment surgery” is also an outdated phrase that’s not commonly used anymore.) Second, have you ever tried to schedule any kind of surgery in this country?! Nothing in health care happens quickly or easily, and that’s not even factoring in maneuvering insurance coverage. It’s also important to remember that there’s no single way to be transgender or nonbinary, and that not every trans or nonbinary person will choose—or be able to afford—a medical transition. These decisions, like all personal and private health care decisions, are best left to patients, their families if applicable, and their health care providers.

Shane Diamond: It is not true that people are claiming to be transgender when they are not. Given how difficult it is to navigate our complicated and tedious health care system and all the discrimination and challenges trans people face, no one is faking being transgender for any advantage—be it in sports or anywhere else.

Out of the millions of global competitive athletes, there are only a handful of transgender people competing at high-level sports, and—despite sensational media narratives—we’re not always winning! It is especially important in the context of youth sports that all kids—transgender and cisgender alike—have an opportunity to participate in sports with their friends to learn valuable, lifelong lessons through being part of a team...without being subjected to gender- and appearance-based discrimination.

Ultimately, these anti-trans actions harm all girls and women, punishing gender non-conformity of any kind and imposing rigid gender roles and expressions on everyone.

Dr. Meredithe McNamara: The American Psychiatric Association doesn’t consider gender dysphoria to be a mental illness—it’s classified as a sexual health condition that arises from discordance between one’s physical body and sense of self. But let’s talk about the word “fix.” How? There is no alternative treatment for gender dysphoria beyond access to thoughtful, multidisciplinary gender-affirming medical care. It’s not right for everyone, but it’s right for many.

Shane Diamond: Being transgender and/or nonbinary is one of nature’s many variations—the way some people have blue eyes, brown eyes, or green eyes. Being transgender is an innate part of who someone is and there’s nothing anyone can do to change that. External forces attempting to change someone’s gender or sexual orientation are ineffective at best and extremely harmful at worst.

Transgender people are not the problem and are simply trying to live safely. The Trump administration has spent millions of dollars vilifying and discriminating against transgender people—why? The American public has many issues of importance, including the economy and climate change, and yet opponents of equality are wasting important resources and attention perpetuating hatred and divisiveness. If anything needs to be fixed, it’s our society's rigid rejection of anyone who is different.

Photos of experts are courtesy of subjects. Answers have been lightly condensed and edited for clarity.

#6662. Lights, Camera, Action! An Exclusive Look Inside ELLE’s 2026 Women in Hollywood Celebration

Florence Pugh, Julianne Moore, Penélope Cruz, and more were honored at our annual event.

Los Angeles has no shortage of awards shows, but ELLE’s annual Women in Hollywood event has always been about something more: putting the spotlight on the women whose work makes the industry worth watching. On Monday night, eight of film and television’s most talked-about talents came together to celebrate their collective achievements, with plenty of oohs, aahs, and well-deserved gushes along the way.

This year’s honorees—Carrie Coon, Elizabeth Debicki, Rosalind Eleazar, Julianne Moore, Samantha Morton, Inde Navarrette, Florence Pugh, and Penélope Cruz—gathered at the Four Seasons Hotel Los Angeles at Beverly Hills for an evening hosted by ELLE editor-in-chief Nina Garcia.

Presented in partnership with Ralph Lauren and Bulgari, with support from Evian, the soirée drew familiar faces from across Tinseltown. Matt Damon, Seth Rogen, Bryce Dallas Howard, Alexander Skarsgård, and Louisa Jacobson were among those who took the stage to present the honors, while Morton accepted hers remotely from her home in London, where she was sick with the flu.

Beyond the eight honorees, the guest list included Lily James, Ashley Park, Elsa Hosk, Madison Beer, Jackie Tohn, Molly Sims, and more.

Champagne and yuzu mocktails flowed as guests mingled before making their way into the ballroom, where a three-course dinner awaited: beet salad, Chilean sea bass, and chocolate tortino.

Garcia kicked things off by celebrating a banner year for moviegoing, while reminding the room that there’s still work to be done when it comes to creating opportunities for women. “We need roles for women that are messy, complicated, funny, and real—and sometimes even older than 50!” she said.

She pointed to the eight honorees as a reminder of what’s possible when those roles make it to the screen. “You are all proof of what happens when these roles are written—you knock it out of the park. These women we are honoring? The characters they have played? They are not decorative. They’re not supporting a man’s journey. They are women in full.”

#6661. Netflix Proved They Are the Masters of Heartland Stories at the Texas Book Festival

The Hunting Wives author May Cobb, bestselling author Katherine Center, and Virgin River showrunner Patrick Sean Smith got real about bringing your favorite cozy stories to life.

A packed theater in Austin, Texas, got together to see three of Netflix’s biggest stars right now—Virgin River showrunner Patrick Sean Smith, bestselling author Katherine Center, and The Hunting Wives author May Cobb—talk about their latest projects at the Texas Book Festival alongside the company’s Vice President of Drama Series, Cole Galvin. Not only is the streamer the home for each and every one of them, but their small-town stories filled with comfort and escapism have become major favorites for fans who simply cannot get enough of it all.

It’s no surprise, especially when you consider how much of an impact that the stories have had on viewers. According to the streamer, “since 2025, book adaptations have driven over 13 billion global views and represented nearly 20% of total view hours.”

May Cobb admits that The Hunting Wives’s success on Netflix—season 2 will be released on November 16—brought her novel to new levels of fandom she’s never seen before.

“Not to where it's in the zeitgeist. Not to where it's a cultural phenomenon, and now it has this incredible life of its own is absolutely staggering to me and I do give all credit to our creator, Rebecca Cutter, and to Netflix,” she admitted.

According to Christine Ball, EVP & Publisher at Berkley, “Originally published in 2021, May Cobb’s The Hunting Wives surged back into the spotlight following its Netflix premiere. In just one week, they have gone back to press twice on the trade paperback and saw a +5,000% increase across all formats in the same one-week period year over year.”

An external consultant noted that in U.S. book sales across physical, audio, and e-books, “lift in the 30 days after Netflix release against the same 30-day period the year prior, and found that, on average, Netflix adaptations increase book sales 10x.”

And it’s not only the authors who are feeling the impact. Even Spotify is getting a nice boost from it with My Life With the Walter Boys bringing a “1,000 percent increase in global listens and over 1,300 percent in global searches on Spotify,” to Ali Novak’s series following the season 1 premiere.

“It's so phenomenal and great because it brings people back to the books,” Katherine Center revealed to Cosmopolitan. Her book, The Bodyguard, will soon be a Netflix film titled Guarding Stars starring Jared Padelecki and Leighton Meester. The film had a sold-out screening at the Texas Book Festival where Katherine and Jared took the stage alongside producer Genevieve Padalecki. It also stars My Life With the Walter Boys star Noah LaLonde, bringing him to another book universe on the streamer.

“There are a lot of people who find out it's going to be a movie and want to read it before they see it,” Katherine continued. “Other people who see the movie, love the movie, and then go back and find the book. It's incredibly helpful.”

And while most streamers are also feeling major successes when it comes to adaptations, it’s clear that Netflix’s focus on comfort/heartland stories is paying off with, “over 20 comfort/heartland titles appearing in the Global top 10 with 6 of them reaching the #1 spot.”

Part of that is how the shows and movies that fill these slots get to make some folks who hardly see themselves on TV finally feel seen.

“I like being able to tell stories for the older generation, just because they're becoming a bit invisible on television,” Patrick Sean Smith said. “And having Tim Matheson and Annette O’Toole to write to is just insane, so that's never an opportunity that I want to squander. We fill out their lives with their peers and their friends and it feels fresh because you don't really see that much on television.”

May Cobb credits Taylor Sheridan’s impact on TV as the reason why we get to see so many of these stories get made away from major metropolitan cities like Los Angeles, New York, and Chicago. It also allowed them to push against the grain with what people might expect from a story based in the Lone Star State.

“What they did that's so interesting with this particular heartland story is they showed this like twistier, more subversive side of Texas that I don't know had been represented yet. I feel like it's not just Texas; it could be small town anywhere,” she noted.

As fans got to check out the Netflix Reading Lounge and pick up copies of their favorite books, it was clear that they all were reacting the same to each title. Options ranged from People We Meet on Vacation to the first volume of One Piece and The Magician’s Assistant, the basis of Greta Gerwig’s highly anticipated Narnia film that will be released in 2027. All books that can offer a bit of comfort in their new read. It helps that the lounge also included bookmark making and free coffee that was perfect to combat the sudden rain and cold that hit Austin during the festival weekend.

“People find me generally when they're going through something hard because they've heard that my books are funny, and they are looking to be lifted up a little bit,” Katherine said. “I hear, I found you after I had surgery. I found you when my mom was sick. I found you after I just lost somebody I loved.”

“There’s this concept in psychology called moral elevation that I think about a lot. It's this idea that when you witness somebody doing something kind for someone else, it gives you this kind of rising feeling in your body and it makes you want to be a better person. I think I'm sort of obsessed with how to create that feeling in a story.”

Patrick notes that he’s witnessed firsthand how these stories can affect even his own cast and crew members during the filming of Virgin River, showing the impact that they have even before fans get to see what happens.

“I've gone through a lot of things in my personal life that the show has helped me move through. I went through a divorce during the wedding season, which was really hard, but was incredibly cathartic,” he revealed. “When we did season five, we did an endometriosis storyline and there were a lot of women that came up and just said, ‘Thank you. I haven't seen this before.’ There's a symbiosis to it.”

And it’s that kind of universality that allows these stories to break down global barriers. According to Netflix, “Comfort/heartland titles have appeared in the Top 10 in 93 different countries. The book adaptations that made our Global Top 10 in 2026 came from 25 different countries, from Nordic crime fiction to an Indian romance novel to Mexican historical fiction.”

May also feels that impact in her home state, especially after the recent elections, noting that there were many memes that linked The Hunting Wives with Blexas’s success.

“No, it was the grassroots, and it was all the hard work,” she said. “I think it's cool how it's ahead of the curve and ahead of its time a little bit. Last week proved that Texas is more diverse in every way.”

It’s the universality that also allows Virgin River, which is set to become Netflix’s longest-running original scripted series thanks to its season 9 renewal, to continue on with such beloved fanfare with endless possibilities.

“I think it can go for as long as the fans continue to show up,” Patrick noted. “It's a place. It's a feel. It's a vibe that people like. We just want to make sure that we keep the storytelling fresh and let these characters grow up and move through life together.”

#6660. Is This the Greatest Fashion Auction of All Time?

3,000 fashion items from a personal collection are up for sale at Maurice Auction in Paris.

Who owns four vintage Dior Maasai necklaces? Not very many people, except for, of course, one of Paris’s most mysterious women and deeply devoted collectors of fashion. The anonymous French clotheshorse is now the subject of an upcoming multipart, 3,000-piece sale at Maurice Auction. “It’s not someone who is known by the industry, it’s just someone who was very wealthy and that could afford such a collection as a true and passionate collector for more than 30 years,” Salomé Pirson, co-founder of Maurice Auction, tells ELLE.

Dubbed the Infinite Wardrobe, the multipart auction contains haute couture, ready-to-wear, handbags, and accessories from the likes of Chanel, Dior, Hermès, Valentino, Roberto Cavalli, Fendi, and Ungaro. The Infinite Wardrobe will kick off with a live auction on November 18 in Paris, followed by eight different online-only auctions sorted by brand or subject (Chanel, Italian fashion, Dior, bags, for instance). Even with the growing interest in auctions recently spurred by a younger demographic, it’s certainly one of the most extensive fashion auctions that has happened in recent years. Earlier this year, Maurice Auction broke records when it sold rare haute couture from the collection of couture queen Mouna Ayoub.

Much of the Infinite Wardrobe is unworn or only worn once, pristinely preserved like a time capsule for decades in one closet. Some of it even still has tags attached. “What’s unusual is the volume,” says Marie-Laurence Tixier, co-founder of Maurice Auction. “The client was really someone who collected, and when she liked a look or a pair of shoes, she would buy all the colors.” For the auction, that means Hermès Oran sandals in every shade of the rainbow and every iteration of vintage Chanel Gripoix necklaces. Some of the items will be sold in more than a dozen variations.

When the duo first encountered the collection, it was stored in a single Parisian apartment, all exhibited by color, type of accessory, and brand. “I’ve heard that people, when they went to have dinner at her place, they visited the closet, because it was a piece of art itself,” adds Tixier. It’s by far the biggest auction the company has ever done since it was founded in 2022, and also the biggest undertaking of their careers in terms of cataloging, researching, and organizing all of the pieces. “It’s like going into a fantasy closet where you realize it’s not gonna be a dream, it’s gonna be a real job for three months,” says Tixier. The rarest items were researched in collaboration with Kerry Taylor, fashion’s favorite London auctioneer.

If you love designer fashion, it’s a great opportunity to pick up a vintage luxury just before the holidays. But for the real fashion freaks, there are some ultra-rare gems that rarely come to market, too. Think: Jean Paul Gaultier-designed Hermès crocodile jackets, Emanuel Ungaro haute couture, and incredible, lightweight vintage Fendi furs. “She had exquisite taste, it’s very elegant, very, very refined,” says Tixier. “There are no logos.”

Roberto Cavalli beaded trousers, Christian Dior by Gianfranco Ferré mini bustle dresses, and Gianni Versace embroidered jackets are just a few of the treasures available in the first part of the auction. In other words: the dream closet is real, and soon, it will be up for grabs.

#6659. I Need Robert Pattinson’s 007 Style to Be the Most Iconic of All the Bonds

There is no one more booked than Robert Pattinson, so it makes perfect sense he’s set to be the next James Bond, too. And if there’s anything I’m sure of, this new iteration of the popular franchise has room to introduce some seriously cool fashion in the suiting department.

Playing 007 comes with some pretty big fashion expectations. We're talking impeccably tailored suits, perfectly cut tuxedos, and very debonair action shots. Now, I’ve only recently ventured into the world of James Bond. My father sat me down a few weeks back and showed me his favorite ones from when he was a kid. But now that Pattinson is entering the world, I’m tuned in. As a diehard Twihard, worlds are colliding with this selection, and that means there’s room for a lot of modern fashion with everyone’s favorite “Gen Z” star at the helm. While Daniel Craig gave us a masterclass in modern Bond tailoring, and Pierce Brosnan brought a little more old-school polish, I need Robert Pattinson to bring a sort of off-kilter edge.

If I were in charge of dressing the next Bond, here's where I think they should start, based on Robert’s previous red carpet suiting.

This striped white suit is surprisingly one of my favorites on him. Now, the fit—while uber beloved by a lot of young people—might get in the way of all his super important action movements. But a loose-fit, light-colored suit really does stand out on the 40-year-old actor. Especially when he has a little tan on him.

Robert has a long-standing partnership with Dior. He is the face of Dior Homme and a notable brand ambassador, and frequently wears custom and seasonal Dior tailored suits on the red carpet and at film premieres. So, we could definitely see some Dior in his upcoming Bond film, given their work together. This shade of suit specifically really brings out the blue in his eyes, so I personally think a little swooning in these action-packed moments will be worthwhile.

Fit-wise, I think this is the style they need to lean into when building out his outfits for the movie. The sharp tailoring and slim fit is not only the definition (to me) of a sharp, modern James Bond, but it also looks great on his frame.

There’s a lot of room to play with menswear, but I also believe there’s room to expand upon the past. I’d also love to see a world where a few newer namesake brands get into the conversation, like Connor Ives and Willy Chavarria. Both brands have a unique POV and make suits fashion people are always talking about. RPats isn’t a typical James Bond. He’s a bit mysterious, but also incredibly online in some regards, so playing up his eccentric personality with some cheeky and edgy suit options would be my dream come true.

Aiyana Ishmael is the style editor at Cosmopolitan magazine. In her work, Aiyana focuses on the culture of fashion and how it intertwines and shapes the zeitgeist. She is an award-winning journalist from Miami, Florida, and a graduate of the historically Black university, Florida A&M. #6655. “Reentry” Is Important Every Day of Your Relationship

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Every time you and your partner say “goodbye”—whether for the day or until your next hangout—you’re not exactly “exiting” the relationship. But, according to sex and relationship experts, you might still have to actively make a “reentry” when you come back together. In fact, it may be something you have to do every single day.

In a recent CNBC Make It article, psychologist Mark Travers says, “‘Reentry moments’ are the first minute or two when a couple comes back together after time apart.” And what you do with these moments can make all the difference when it comes to how you and your partner connect in your relationship.

When we’re deep in a relationship—whether because you’re a few years in or live together—we sometimes go into autopilot when we see each other again. We might get home after a long day of work and just continue with our evening routine while maneuvering around our partners, rather than with them. And while it’s totally okay to decompress after the day by yourself or even spend some evenings just doing our own thing, it’s still important to make a conscious effort to connect with our partners.

“Oftentimes, we are wearing so much protective armor to navigate the outside world and we don't even realize it,” Anastacia Sams, LMFT tells Cosmopolitan. “There is a certain way that we need to show up with our jobs, our friends, or in our other responsibilities, that we forget to lay our armor down and connect with our partner, human to human.”

LaTrease Nwosu, LPC explains that everyday reentry can look like giving each other a meaningful hug (one that lasts longer than 30 seconds), sharing a kiss, or—most effectively—asking them how their day was. It can look like just being curious about each other.

Reentry looks different for every couple depending on their core values and relationship goals. Sams explains that for couples that value good conversation, a 30-minute debrief about your day or something you learned is a great way to connect. For others, something as simple as an acknowledgement—“I miss you,” or “I’m looking forward to reconnecting later”—can make a partner feel secure and attended to.

“Small moments that show your consideration of one another add up, and reentry is the best time to do this,” Sams says.

If you and your partner already plan date nights and have uninterrupted quality time together, you might think that “reentry” isn’t going to make much of a difference. But a relationship is a continuous conversation. And sure, you might have a romantic date night once a month, but they shouldn’t feel like the only time you really feel close to your partner. “Reentry” can help maintain the connection every day of the relationship.

“When couples consistently skip these moments, it creates feelings of disconnection that lead to resentment,” Sams warns. “This is when couples start to feel they are just passing by each other throughout the day, or starting to feel like they are more roommates than romantic partners.”

She explains that over time, skipping these moments wear at the emotional safety of the relationship. Your partner may feel less inclined to share details about their life or take part in other forms of intimacy if this baseline of connection isn’t achieved.

But the good thing is that “reentry” isn’t the responsibility of one person in the relationship—both partners can work to initiate these moments so that it doesn’t always feel like only one person is trying to reconnect. Sams explains that “reentry” needs to be reciprocal in the sense that their needs to be mutual care, mutual respect, and mutual consideration between partners—but you don’t have to literally take turns checking in on each other.

“Either partner can initiate these moments of reconnection, and it is important for both partners to prioritize the intentionality of these moments without turning it into something that's transactional,” Sams says.

There are going to be days when you want to be alone, are having an argument with your partner, or are simply too overwhelmed to think about anything but your to-do list. Even in these times, I’d challenge you to keep “reentry” consistent. It doesn’t always have to be something that doesn’t honor your feelings, but being an attentive partner even when you’re tempted not to goes a very long way in relationships. It creates mutual respect and will encourage your partner to lead with consideration as well. No matter what’s going on in your life or as hectic as simply existing gets, remember that you chose to partner with this person for a reason. And by making an effort to “reenter” the relationship after time apart will do wonders for your connection.

#6654. How to Reset Your Skin Care Routine After 40

Dermatologists break down the ingredients, steps, and treatments that matter most for skin over 40.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As we age, there’s seemingly always something new to think about, including adjusting your skin care routine to meet your skin’s evolving needs. You may not need to add a bunch of new products to your current regimen, but increased dryness, changes in texture, fine lines, and enlarged pores may require ingredient swaps that your current lineup can’t deliver. The skin care products you used in your 20s and 30s may have worked then, but once you hit 40, your skin may start to need something different. Experts share how to reset your routine for 40+ skin, including the non-negotiables, new ingredients that can have the greatest impact, and what to avoid to keep your maturing skin looking and feeling its best.

Over 40, skin has already started to thin, at both the epidermis and dermis levels, explains Dr. Suzan Obagi, a board-certified dermatologist, associate professor of dermatology and plastic surgery, and director of the UPMC Cosmetic Surgery & Skin Health Center. “This means that skin will start to appear more fragile, fine lines will start to show, and skin becomes drier. For these reasons, a good skin care regimen should include a retinoid (retinol, retinaldehyde, or tretinoin) to help improve skin thickness, increase extracellular matrix proteins (collagen, elastin, and glycosaminoglycans (GAGs)), and encourage fibroblasts to make more of our Natural Moisturizing Factor (NMF),” Obagi tells ELLE.

Most skin over 40 also has some sun damage, which Obagi says can present as freckles and sun spots. Well before 40 and certainly after, there’s one daily non-negotiable for skin of any age: sunscreen. If the spots have already shown up, Obagi recommends a skin-brightening serum with tranexamic acid (TXA), antioxidants, and peptides to help correct the hyperpigmentation.

Additionally, Dr. Chris Tomassian, a California-based, board-certified dermatologist and founder of The Dermatology Collective, says a retinol at night will go a long way, as will vitamin C in the morning and aggressive moisturization.

As for ingredients, Tomassian says the key players in the post-40 reset will be retinol, SPF, and vitamin C, as already noted, along with PDRN, ectoin, ceramides, and occasionally estradiol.

If you’re not already on a daytime and nighttime routine, now’s the time to start.

Morning: Use a gentle cleanser if your skin is oily or if you didn’t get to a proper wash the night before. Tomassian suggests skipping the cleanse if your skin feels dry when you wake up. Follow with a good antioxidant like an L-ascorbic acid (vitamin C) serum or one with tetrahexyldecyl ascorbate (THD), silymarin, or phloretin. Layer on a PDRN moisturizer and SPF before any makeup.

Night: Before bed, cleanse thoroughly to remove makeup and sunscreen. Layer on a copper peptide serum, ptiox treatment, tretinoin (retinol), and a PDRN moisturizer. Obagi also suggests combining a pigment-correcting serum with a retinoid to help build the dermis extracellular matrix and target hyperpigmentation.

Bonus steps: Exfoliate one to two times each week with a combination peel pad, alpha hydroxy or beta hydroxy acids depending on your skin’s dryness level or if you’re experiencing any breakout. Tomassian warns, though, that post-40 skin should avoid over-exfoliating. “Skin gets drier with age, and breaking down your skin barrier by scrubbing or peeling too aggressively or too often can leave skin vulnerable to drying out,” he explains.

At any age, consistency is key. Even if you’re lucky enough to have minimal signs of aging, both experts agree that these steps will go a long way. “It is still important to incorporate a retinoid, an antioxidant, and a good moisturizer into your daily routine,” says Obagi.

There’s plenty you can do at home to care for your skin after 40, including adding LED light therapy to your lineup to help with fine lines and wrinkles and stimulate collagen—but Tomassian notes these are only beneficial if you use them consistently. An even greater investment is pairing your home routine with regular visits to a professional for in-office procedures like microneedling or ablative and nonablative lasers. These treatments can jump-start your skin to start producing more collagen. For chronic sun damage like redness and dark spots, Tomassian recommends Intense Pulsed Light (IPL), Picosure, or DermaV/Fraxel/CO2 lasers. These devices target pigmentation concerns that skin care does little to improve.

Obagi adds that pro visits are important since collagen production peaks at age 18 and, with each passing year, our skin makes 1 percent less collagen, elastin, and GAGs. “By our mid-30s and early 40s, we would benefit from devices that boost collagen production periodically. These can be noninvasive lasers, microneedling, and radiofrequency devices. These will boost collagen for a short time, but when combined with a well-thought-out skin care regimen, the results can be prolonged,” says Obagi.

You’ve lived with your skin for 40+ years, so you can tell when things start to change. Obagi says you have to listen to your own skin. “If you are on the dry side or have fragile skin, avoid harsh ingredients such as glycolic or lactic acid, strong retinoids, or over-exfoliating with scrubs. These can irritate the skin and drive sensitivity,” she highlights. If you have thicker skin or are oilier, she suggests exfoliating fruit acids and says your skin can tolerate stronger retinoids.

Tomassian’s rule of thumb is that after age 30, use as many ingredients as possible to bank collagen and focus on thicker moisturizers as you get older, since we produce less oil.

Key ingredients: oat-derived surfactants, plant-derived humectants, polyhydroxy acids

Key ingredients: liposomal tranexamic acid (TXA), oligopeptide-68, THD, and hydrolyzed algin to help target the various pathways driving pigmentation

Key ingredients: stabilized vitamin C, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, soothing allantoin, and green tea extract

Key ingredients: collagen, copper peptides, smoothing and brightening niacinamide, retinal, rice PDRN

#6653. Tom Holland and Zendaya Wore a Couple of Fantastic Gold Watches to the Governors Awards

The timepieces themselves? Drastically different. The vibes? Entirely in sync.

Tom Holland and Zendaya were among the stars attending this past weekend’s Governors Awards in Los Angeles, where both made interesting choices when it came to their watches.

Holland, dressed in a black double-breasted Louis Vuitton dinner suit, white shirt, and black bow tie—with tan leather Christian Louboutin boots—was photographed pulling back his cuff to reveal a rose-gold Tank Louis Cartier on a leather strap.

Introduced in 1922, it's one of watchmaking's great enduring designs, with a list of famous admirers that includes Andy Warhol, Yves Saint Laurent, and Princess Diana.

Zendaya, meanwhile, went vintage. Her choice was a small, chic, solid-gold Rolex dating from the 1960s, believed to be the very watch she bought at a vintage market in her hometown of Oakland just over a week ago.

According to the San Francisco Chronicle, the actress spotted the watch at a stall run by independent dealer Wrist Glow Co., tried it on, went off to browse and returned an hour later to buy it. The price? A little over $6,000.

Watch-spotters have identified the Rolex as a ladies' cocktail watch, reference 8196, although that identification has yet to be independently confirmed.

So, what have we learned? Perhaps that for two stars who do old-school Hollywood glamour so well, there are few better choices for an awards-night watch than a classic in gold. Which one you go for, though, remains a matter of personal taste.

#6650. Florence Pugh Credits the ‘Thousands of People’ Who Made Her Career Possible

Florence Pugh is making sure every person who helped make her career possible gets their due. In her Women in Hollywood speech, the Dune: Part Three star reflected on what a “bonkers ride” it is to be a woman in the industry. “Being bold and powerful and opinionated—always opinionated—and then vulnerable whenever the cameras need, is a skill I’m still in awe of all of us being able to do,” she said. “Thank you, ELLE, for honoring all of these powerhouse women.”

She went on to acknowledge the “thousands of people” who helped make her career possible. Being an actress was a childhood dream. “When I was child, I was asked what I wanted to be when I grew up, and it was this,” she recalled. “But I didn’t know what ‘this’ was, though. I just saw the women and men on my TV and would feel supremely jittery and thrilled watching them. I would get close to the screen, so close I could feel the warmth and static and study all of their faces. I would mainly get jealous of men actually, and weirdly, he’s in my speech, Matt Damon, I was just always jealous of him. And I don’t know why! I don’t! He’s got it all!

“I knew I wanted to do that,” she continued. “All of it, the bells, the whistles. The ability to be free and perform and live in make-believe. The thing is, I wasn’t aware that the biggest and best thing about working in this industry was yet to come. Yes, the explosions and close-ups and locations and free breakfast, lunch, and dinner is cool, but when I truly sit and think about how I got here, it’s down to the thousands of people who have been a part of my career all the way through. They made the path smooth so I could climb to the top of the mountain and wave my flag, which is what I am doing right now. My career and existence in this job is down to thousands of people.

“I think of the crews I’ve been lucky enough to work with, who create the space and the stage so I can do what I do comfortably. I think of the casting directors at the beginning of my career, who took a risk arguing for me, claiming that it didn’t matter that I had no qualifications and only one film credit. Or for the dressers, who would fib and say there was a costume malfunction when they could see I hadn’t had enough time to eat my lunch—this was pre-SAG membership. So don’t panic, SAG. Or to the glam teams all over the world who join because they believe in you and what you do. The makeup artists and hair stylists who wash and clean brushes well into the night just for you, tomorrow at 5 A.M.”

“I think of all of these people who protected me on my journey,” she said. “And it would not have been possible without every single one of them. Just in case you’re listening now and you’re not aware how grateful we are and how important you are, I just received an ELLE Women of the Year honor, and I didn’t get here on my own. I only took stock in myself because I could see you doing it for me first.”

Watch Pugh’s full speech above and read her Women in Hollywood cover story here.

#6648. Mashed Potatoes

No holiday table—or really any dinner table, for that matter—is truly complete without a substantial side of creamy, buttery mashed potatoes. While it may seem like simple, classic comfort food, there are a few ways to take mashed potatoes from good to great. That's why we retested this recipe again and again until we achieved potato perfection.

What People Are Saying:"So good! I don’t typically like sour cream on its own but it was a huge improvement for basic mashed potatoes. Will definitely be making this again." – PurpleStrawberry

"Easily my go-to mashed potato recipe. I've made it for Thanksgiving, and got requests to make it again. It's so good, doesn't even need gravy." – kitkat123

Really, the only chopping you'll have to do is cut up the potatoes. I do suggest peeling them (more on that later), and be sure to try to cut the potatoes into equal-sized pieces so they cook evenly. Around 1" chunks are ideal. Time to cook 'em! In testing, I found that covering the potatoes with cold water first yields the best consistency and ensures the potatoes don't become too mushy. Don't forget to add a pinch of salt to the water! Let the potatoes cook until softened—the best way to test is to use a paring knife and make sure it can be inserted with no resistance. This should take about 20 minutes. Make sure to drain when they're done so they don't overcook.

Now comes the fun part: Mashing the potatoes until smooth. You can use a masher, a ricer, or even a handheld mixer for this step. I think the smoother, the better, but you do you, of course.

On to what makes these potatoes the best ever: Heat up some whole milk and butter until the butter is melted in a pot.

Then, pour over the still-warm potatoes and mix to combine. Then add the sour cream, some salt, and pepper, and you're basically good to go! Mix everything well, transfer to your prettiest serving bowl, and proceed with the finishing touches: a couple of pieces of butter to melt into the potatoes, plus fresh parsley for some color and brightness. Brace yourself for the compliments.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

If you have any leftovers, store them in an airtight container in the fridge for a max of 3 to 4 days. With all of our recipes to transform your creamy leftover mashed potatoes—like fried mashed potato balls, potato rolls, waffles... we doubt they'll even last that long.

#6646. I Tried The Great British Bake Off’s Most Controversial Recipe—Here’s How It Went

The “technical challenge,” a blind-baking test where contestants receive the same recipe and ingredients, often with minimal or deliberately sparse instructions, is a staple of The Great British Bake Off. And contestants have seriously struggled with it over the years. But according to Paul Hollywood, this season’s challenge featuring blue cheese and roasted grape flatbreads was the worst yet. After 60 minutes of kneading, grape-roasting, and undercooking, the bakers produced trays of thick, raw flatbreads that Paul and Nigella could barely eat.

After bakers argued that an hour wasn’t enough, Paul said, “There’s plenty of time.” But fans, critics, and former bakers alike have been plenty vocal about their feelings. The popular opinion? The bakers were set up to fail—and also that the flavors seemed a little weird.

With all the controversy, I was curious to see if the challenge was even possible—so I tried it myself. While I purposefully did NOT watch the episode before my attempt, I did print the full recipe from the GBBO website. This recipe was definitely WAY more helpful than the one the bakers were given, and I followed it to the letter.

Here’s how it went: the good, the bad, and the very ugly.

The beginning? Very smooth. I put the grapes in the oven (the online recipe told me to roast them as a whole bunch rather than cutting them up) and made the bread dough, kneading it for the five minutes specified before letting it rest.

As soon as I set the dough aside to rest, I discovered how absolutely torturous technical downtime is. I was at an absolute loss. I had nothing to do…except read the recipe over and over again and make sure I knew what 8 cm and 23 cm looked like.

Interestingly enough, the bakers on the show didn’t have this downtime; they were busy cutting and roasting their grapes by then. That said, I don’t think that order was necessarily better; with the time spent slicing the grapes, they wouldn’t have had much time at ALL to roast—and not nearly enough to cool.

The grapes came out of the oven. They weren’t very roasted—as one of the bakers said, they just looked like “raw grapes”—but the recipe said 20 minutes and gave no indicators for how roasted they should be (and also I knew they needed to cool before stuffing), so I just went with it.

I dumped out the dough (a rude awakening after its 15-minute rest) and chopped it into eight pieces. Immediately, I realized I should have weighed the larger ball and divided it by eight so I could make sure all the pieces were equal, but I didn’t have much time to fuss, so I tried to make them roughly the same weight and moved on.

The recipe said to roll out each portion of dough into an 8 cm circle, fill it with five roasted grapes and one-eighth of the blue cheese, lift the edges of the circle up to pinch them together, then turn the little ball seam side down and roll the whole thing out into a 23 cm circle.

To begin, I cut my dough into wedges from the larger ball, and it was hard to roll them out into a circle to fill. But that was nothing compared to the problems I had rolling out the filled dough. First, the dough wasn’t rested enough, so it kept springing back at me as I tried to roll it out. But the bigger issue was the grapes. Since they were still pretty juicy, they started bursting as I rolled out the flatbread, flying to the edges and tearing holes in the dough as they oozed out.

After over 15 minutes of stuffing and rolling, my workstation was littered with burst grape guts. None of my flatbreads were 23 cm wide (I think the widest they got was 18?) and I still had three to roll out, but I also knew I needed to start cooking or I was going to have eight totally raw flatbreads at the end of time.

Cooking the flatbreads wasn’t a total disaster—I guess—but grape juice oozed out of the holes where they burst and left black patches of burnt sugar on the pan, which I had absolutely no time to clean out since I was still racing the clock. Fortunately, the flatbreads didn’t seem to need the full 90 seconds per side. In fact, they seemed to need constant attention to prevent them from burning, which was bad news since I was still trying to roll out the remaining three flatbreads on the side.

Time was up, and I had five cooked (burst) stuffed flatbreads on my sheet tray, three totally raw ones on the countertop, and a smoking pan. I now know how the bakers feel at the end of such challenges: slightly insane and utterly defeated.

Looking back on the challenge, I can honestly say that it was difficult and somewhat impossible. Ultimately, I couldn’t get all the flatbreads cooked in time, even though I was rolling them out much thinner than the bakers were. But while timing was definitely a problem, the bigger issue was the impossible stupidity of stuffing juicy grapes inside a flatbread and then trying to roll it out without the grapes bursting out and ruining the dough.

Since I managed to cook five flatbreads, I could (unlike Paul and Nigella) taste the finished result—but honestly, I wasn’t a fan. The blue cheese was WAY too strong, and the grapes felt like random pockets of juice in an otherwise savory flatbread. Overall, I wouldn’t recommend trying this challenge yourself—unless you want to be scrubbing the burnt bits of grape sugar off your pan for the next week.

#6645. Beyond the Blueprint: The Art of Building an Architectural Legacy

What does it take to create something that truly endures? How can luxury leaders balance heritage with innovation and create spaces that are not only beautiful but meaningful to the people who inhabit them? Against the backdrop of London’s iconic Tower Bridge, some of the leading names in luxury gathered aboard one of the world’s most extraordinary superyachts, Kismet, for an afternoon spent discussing the legacies they hope to leave behind.

These questions brought together influential leaders across fashion, jewelry, architecture, real estate, retail, and technology for an exclusive Vogue Business gathering in collaboration with Shanna Collective. Held on October 14, with London's historic skyline as its setting and the superyacht itself offering a compelling example of ambitious design, the event explored what it takes to create spaces, brands, and experiences that transcend generations. Hosted by Shanna Khan, founder and CEO of Shanna Collective, the afternoon opened with remarks from Vogue Business head of advisory and custom insights Anusha Couttigane, followed by a panel moderated by Vogue Business features director Lucy Maguire. Speakers included Sergei Mosunov, CEO of Fabergé; Jade Holland Cooper, founder and creative director of Holland Cooper; and Dr Valerie Vaughan-Dick MBE, chief executive of the Royal Institute of British Architects (RIBA).

“Hosting this gathering with Vogue Business aboard Kismet was a wonderful opportunity to convene thought leaders from architecture, hospitality, real estate, and retail, each bringing a distinct perspective on the future of design and placemaking to build an enduring legacy,” said Khan. “What struck me was the shared understanding that creating something truly exceptional requires more than a vision for the space itself; it requires an understanding of its wider context, its people, and the legacy it can leave behind. Anusha and I were delighted to bring this group together, and I left feeling inspired by the ambition, openness, and collective thinking in the room,” she added.

Left to right: Lucy Maguire (features director, Vogue Business), Jade Holland Cooper (founder and CEO, Holland Cooper), Anusha Couttigane (head of advisory and custom insights, Vogue Business), Shanna Khan (founder and CEO, Shanna Collective) and Dr. Valerie Vaughan-Dick MBE (CEO, RIBA).

Opening the discussion, Couttigane drew parallels between London’s architectural history and the role of private enterprise in shaping cities.

“If you look out across the city, you can observe centuries of architectural ambition, entrepreneurship, and reinvention.” she began. “It’s a reminder that the great cities we inherit today weren’t shaped by architects and politicians alone. They were also shaped by entrepreneurs, investors, and business leaders who saw opportunities to create something bigger than themselves — investing in the physical landscape, advancing commerce and culture, and creating places that would serve communities for generations,” she continued.

The English capital also holds a symbolic place in Khan’s business philosophy. “It was no coincidence that we gathered in the heart of London, a city that shaped that modern world and one that hosted us for discussions on how we can collectively steward the future,” Khan said. Having recently been interviewed by Couttigane for Vogue Business, Khan’s own work includes the regeneration of Chicago’s Fulton Market district and Jacksonville’s Shipyards development, where design, commerce, and community intersect.

“What I find particularly compelling about Shanna’s approach is her conviction that exceptional design should do more than create beautiful buildings and interiors. It should contribute something enduring to the places and people around it. It should honor the past and the history of a place, while making it relevant for modern users. It’s a philosophy that resonates strongly with our discussion today,” Couttigane added.

Anusha Couttigane delivering the opening remarks.

Beginning the panel, Maguire asked Mosunov how Fabergé is translating the hallmarks of the brand, such as craftsmanship, detail, and surprise into the physical realm, given its ambitions to expand physically. “The previous owner had a business model that was focused on B2B. It didn’t have its own retail distribution. When I bought Fabergé, I decided that we needed to expand the direct-to-consumer business, with the main instrument being our own boutiques,” Mosunov explained.

The challenge is creating spaces that communicate the maison’s heritage while supporting its contemporary ambitions. “We needed to express this historical story through the boutiques, showcasing pre-1970 pieces and sharing the story about Peter Carl Fabergé, the founder of the company. This way, you get a real sense of the company.” Mosunov also shared how the brand is designing jewelry iterations of its iconic egg motifs, inspired by the fifty Imperial Eggs that secured the maison’s acclaim between 1885 and 1916.

As Fabergé expands its international retail footprint, Mosunov is adapting the design of its boutiques to local preferences. “For example, in the Middle East, we have a store in the Dubai Mall. They like very opulent forms in that region so we need to adapt the execution to the local marketplace.”

Vaughan-Dick reinforced the importance of cultural context in architecture. Referencing Norman Foster’s work in the Middle East, she highlighted the influence of climate, environment and local requirements on architectural decisions. “You cannot have this homogenous view of design. You have to think about culture, sustainability, and building safety. You have to look at the different requirements for that country and for that particular society,” she added.

Turning to Holland Cooper, Maguire asked about how she was creating environments that would resonate with the world of the brand. As a business that has grown from stalls at Badminton Horse Trials to a £100m+ company with flagship boutiques and a new 70,000-square-foot HQ in the Cotswolds, Holland Cooper spoke about the importance of making the brand experience as special as possible.

Left to right: Lucy Maguire (features director, Vogue Business), Dr. Valerie Vaughan-Dick MBE (CEO, RIBA), Sergei Mosunov (owner and CEO, Fabergé) and Jade Holland Cooper (founder and CEO, Holland Cooper).

“Although we don’t have a lot of shops, the ones that we do have are so important.” she explained. “For me, the importance of retail is making that physical experience as special as we possibly can. I think there’s a narrative that retail is dead, that bricks and mortar is dead, but I think we’re just getting too lazy about the experience that we’re creating for the customer…what we’ve done in our physical retail spaces is really understand that the service and environment are paramount to that experience,” she added.

Holland Cooper’s retail philosophy draws inspiration from brands including Ralph Lauren and Abercrombie & Fitch, whose distinctive environments left a lasting impression on her as a young shopper: “I am pedantic about music volume, lighting, smell, hanger presentation, visual merchandising, how the staff and the service levels are, because the minute you walk through those doors, you want it to be immersive, and you want people to forget the outside world. That’s why they call it retail therapy.”

Maguire then highlighted a tension in the theme: “Buildings need to endure, but the people, communities, and lifestyles they serve are constantly changing. How should brands, architects and developers design for longevity without becoming trapped by the tastes or assumptions of the present?” she asked.

For Vaughan-Dick, the guiding architectural principle is “long life, loose fit”: creating buildings capable of adapting to future needs. “I do think that individuals recognize that when you’re designing buildings, they don’t know what a building is going to look like in 100 years time, but every architect, every engineer, everyone here needs to think: what we design now needs to have that longer life,” she stated.

Left to right: Tom Pascall (communications manager, the Chancery Rosewood), Joanna Gunn (chief brand officer, Rosewood), Shanna Khan (CEO and founder, Shanna Collective) and Naomi Sebley (the Chancery Rosewood).

For Mosunov, Fabergé’s design legacy distinguishes between timeless beauty, modern beauty, and more transient aesthetic preferences when considering its future. Holland Cooper similarly sees heritage as an enduring source of differentiation: “I am obsessive about heritage, classic shapes and just reinventing them to be appealing to the modern audience, and that doesn’t mean ripping the rulebook up entirely. I pay homage to all the beautiful British aesthetics, and that’s the romanticism of what we’re doing, really,” said Holland Cooper.

As a consequence, British manufacturing remains fundamental to the brand’s identity and ambitions. “All of our tailoring is made in London. All of the wool is woven in the UK, and I’ve done that since day one. And I was told that it wouldn’t be possible to scale a business and do that, and I have, and it is. And I’m really passionate about the UK manufacturing industry. So that’s a big part of our heritage,” she explained.

For Khan, these principles of detail, experience and emotional connection are paramount: “Jade shared her belief that every detail matters to drive an exuberant retail experience. Elements that may seem subtle influence how a place is experienced, remembered, and valued. For me, that is where design becomes truly meaningful: when we can transport guests in time and place, it moves beyond aesthetics to create an emotional connection that endures long after the initial experience itself.”

Left to right: Andrea Latten, (chief business officer, Condé Nast Britain), Shanna Khan (CEO and founder, Shanna Collective) and Christopher Warren (UK head of industry, Condé Nast Britain).

The afternoon concluded with a tour of Kismet, whose interior design was led by Shanna as chief creative director. The superyacht is an example of the extraordinary experiences that thoughtful design can create. For an industry deeply rooted in heritage, the gathering raised a pertinent question: what are today’s luxury leaders creating that will remain relevant and meaningful to future generations?

For Shanna Khan, working together across disciplines is central to answering this question. “Our collaboration with Vogue Business creates a valuable opportunity to bring different disciplines into our conversations and explore how ideas from across creative landscapes can inform one another. The future of luxury lies in creating places and experiences with substance, soul, and generational value,” she said. But she reflected: “Ultimately, building in an enduring way is defined by creating something that feels relevant today, salutes the heritage of the past, and has the potential to become part of a legacy for generations to come.”

#6644. All the Photos of Princess Kate and Prince William on Their Overseas Tour of Mumbai

The royal pair will spend three days in India for the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards

On Monday, November 16, Princess Kate and Prince William landed in Mumbai, India, for their first overseas royal tour in four years. The last time the pair made an international state visit was when they traveled to the Caribbean in the spring of 2022 to celebrate the late Queen Elizabeth II’s Platinum Jubilee.

Now, the Prince and Princess of Wales have hit the road again for the 2026 Earthshot Prize Awards, which are being hosted in Asia for the first time ever. The annual prize, founded by Prince William with an inaugural ceremony in 2021 and set to last until 2030, celebrates innovators who are breaking ground in the environmentalism space.

While it was announced back in February that William would be traveling for the ceremony, it was confirmed only this past weekend that Kate would be joining him. This marks her first major trip with William since her 2024 cancer diagnosis and eventual remission (she also took a solo trip to Italy this May).

The 2026 Earthshot ceremony takes place on November 17, but Kate and William are spending a total of three days in India. They began their trip with a visit to Ballard Estate, the business and shopping district of Mumbai. There, the Prince and Princess of Wales played a bit of gully cricket, took a ride on the metro, and visited local vendors and performers. Then, on day two, they stopped by the Bandra Worli Sea Link promenade and attended the Children’s Earthshot ceremony before dressing up for the official event.

For the rest of their time abroad, the royals will be “celebrating innovation, sustainability, youth leadership, and the enduring connections between the United Kingdom and India,” per their office at Kensington Palace.

Ahead, see all the best photos of Kate and William from their overseas tour of Mumbai.

#6642. ‘Dancing With the Stars’ Will Air Finale Live on West Coast for First Time Ever

Dancing With the Stars is making a major change heading into the finale next Tuesday! The show will air its three-hour finale live on both the East Coast and the West Coast, instead of only going live on the East Coast and having West Coast viewers watch the show late and risk being spoiled.

According to Deadline, the Dancing With the Stars finale will air Tuesday, November 24, at 8 p.m. ET and 5 p.m. PT. It will then re-air at 8 p.m. PT for everyone on the West Coast unable to catch the live viewing.

Of course, this means that an enormous amount of viewers on the West Coast will be able to vote live in the finale for the first time ever, which could definitely influence the outcome of the show!

So, why doesn’t Dancing With the Stars air live on both the West and East Coasts every week? Showrunner Conrad Green told The Hollywood Reporter that it conflicts with local news on the West Coast. As he put it, “local news and the coveted primetime programming slots” get in the way of a live show—but there’s also an issue with rights.

“It’s a big issue for [affiliates] is if everyone starts watching the show at 5 p.m. when it airs,” he said. “If you’re in L.A. and you watch at 5 p.m. when it airs [at 8 p.m.] on the East Coast, then it’s going to massively affect the people who will watch the show when it comes over to these different territories and those markets. The affiliates are hugely important in the bigger picture for the overall economic structure of the show and for the history of ABC and all of those things.”

Clearly ABC made an exception for the finale!

#6641. Classic Stuffing

While traditional stuffing is technically supposed to be stuffed inside the turkey to cook, that isn’t actually the ideal or even recommended way to cook your Thanksgiving turkey (or stuffing). I've found that you’ll achieve much better results by cooking them separately. By cooking your stuffing in a baking dish, you get crunchy, buttery pieces of bread on top and soft, bread pudding-esque pieces underneath. It's my perfect version of the holiday side—I tested it again and again, so it was worthy of your Thanksgiving table.

What People Are Saying:"[It's] an excellent recipe you can tweak it for taste. I like to up the onion by half again and use a lot more fresh sage." - GreenClover

"Using a [mandoline] made the final preparation fast and easy. It was a big hit over a lovely meal. The dressing brought high praise from two trained chefs." - RedFish

As I mentioned above, sourdough bread is my go-to for stuffing. The first step is to bake it! You'll need 2 sheets, or do this in batches. You want to dry it out, so bake for 15 to 20 minutes.

While that's toasting, it's time to cook the aromatics. Melt some butter, then cook the onions, leeks, and garlic until your kitchen smells amazing and everything is softened.

Time for the liquids: Simply whisk eggs, broth, milk, and some fresh herbs in a large measuring cup. You can use a touch of red pepper for a hint of spice, which I highly recommend, but if you have a spice-averse crowd, you can skip it.

When the bread is done toasting, turn the oven temp up to 375°. Prepare a 13" x 9" baking dish by coating it with a thin layer of butter.

Time to combine it all together: In a large bowl, mix the bread, aromatics, and liquid ingredients and toss until combined. You can let this sit at room temperature for up to 1 hour if you're planning ahead. Dot with even more butter, then bake until crispy. You can serve this stuffing warm or at room temperature.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

This classic stuffing is our no-frills, go-to recipe, but we've got plenty of options if you like to get creative with this staple Thanksgiving side. Here are a few of my favorites:

Keep any leftovers covered in the refrigerator for up to 4 days. Reheat portions in the oven at 350° until crisp and warmed through. Looking for leftover ideas? Our Thanksgiving leftovers casserole and Thanksgiving potato skins are here for you.
